[Director 指令] [Patch] K=4 3-seed 数据整合 — 论文最终更新（DDL 2026-05-25）

K=4 multi-seed 全部完成。这是提交前最后一批重大修改。这次不用验证引用。

## 数据

K=4 3-seed downstream:
| Task | K=4 Mean±Std | K=1 Mean | E2E Mean | Genuine Coupling% |
|------|-------------|----------|----------|-------------------|
| PPL | 8.89±0.01 | 9.05 | 8.81 | 33% |
| ARC-c | 40.0±1.3 | 39.9 | 43.7 | 97% |
| WG | 66.9±0.2 | 65.9 | 68.9 | 67% |
| MMLU | 42.7±1.4 | 38.6 | 40.7 | >100% (anomalous) |
| GSM8K | 7.0±1.6 | 5.8 | 7.5 | 29% |
| BoolQ | 67.7±7.5 | 64.5 | 73.7 | 65% (unreliable, std=7.5) |

## 修改清单

### 1. §4.5 L161-163 完全改写（最高优先级）

替换当前 "A $K{=}4$ pilot..." 段落为：

```latex
The $K{=}1$ downstream gap conflates true coupling with train--inference mismatch (training masks 1/32 layers; inference applies all 32). A 3-seed $K{=}4$ control\footnote{Actual sparsity 29.6\%; per-layer sparsity std=0.003 (near-uniform).} decomposes this confound (Appendix~\ref{app:k4_downstream}): \emph{coupling-dominated}---ARC-c retains 97\% of the residual gap (40.0$\pm$1.3 vs.\ E2E 43.7$\pm$2.5) and WG retains 67\% (66.9$\pm$0.2 vs.\ 68.9$\pm$0.4), indicating genuine cross-layer benefits for commonsense reasoning; \emph{mismatch-dominated}---PPL (67\% closure) and GSM8K (71\% closure) gaps largely close; \emph{anomalous}---MMLU exceeds E2E KL across all seeds (42.7$\pm$1.4 vs.\ 40.7$\pm$1.3), attributable to $K{=}4$'s near-uniform learned sparsity matching uniform eval allocation, independently confirmed by a per-layer constraint experiment (Appendix~\ref{app:constraint}).
```

关键改动 vs 当前文本：
- "pilot" → "3-seed control"
- "suggests" → "decomposes/confirms"
- 脚注从 "Single seed (s42)" → "Actual sparsity 29.6%; per-layer sparsity std=0.003"
- BoolQ 从主文移除（方差过大）
- GSM8K 从 "no benefit" 修正为 "mismatch-dominated (71% closure)"
- MMLU 加 constraint 交叉验证

### 2. Abstract L5 修改

当前："coupling scope contributes 21--48\% on downstream tasks; a $K{=}4$ pilot suggests the downstream gap is partly a train--inference mismatch artifact, with task-dependent structure."

改为："coupling scope contributes 10--47\% on downstream tasks (3-seed $K{=}4$-corrected; commonsense 22--47\%, reasoning 10\%); a train--inference mismatch confound in the $K{=}1$ control inflates the $K{=}1$ upper bound (21--48\%)."

### 3. Conclusion L4 修改

当前："cross-layer coupling contributes 21--48\% on downstream tasks, though a $K{=}4$ pilot suggests part of this gap reflects train--inference mismatch (Appendix~\ref{app:k4_downstream})."

改为："cross-layer coupling contributes 10--47\% on downstream tasks (3-seed $K{=}4$-corrected; commonsense 22--47\%, GSM8K 10\%); MMLU anomalously favors $K{=}4$, reflecting allocation alignment confirmed by constraint analysis (Appendices~\ref{app:k4_downstream},~\ref{app:constraint})."

### 4. Introduction L22 修改

当前 contribution #1 中 "coupling scope (21--48\% on downstream tasks)"

改为："coupling scope (10--47\% on downstream tasks, $K{=}4$-corrected)"

### 5. Appendix K=4 Table 更新（Table 14 或相应编号）

全部更新为 3-seed means±std。删除 "Single seed" 标注。新 table:

```latex
\begin{tabular}{lcccc}
\toprule
& $K{=}4$ (3-seed) & $K{=}1$ (3-seed) & E2E KL (3-seed) & Gap closure (\%) \\
\midrule
PPL$\downarrow$ & 8.89$\pm$0.01 & 9.05$\pm$0.02 & 8.81$\pm$0.02 & 66.7 \\
ARC-c & 40.0$\pm$1.3 & 39.9 & 43.7$\pm$2.5 & 2.6 \\
WG & 66.9$\pm$0.2 & 65.9 & 68.9$\pm$0.4 & 33.3 \\
MMLU$^\ast$ & 42.7$\pm$1.4 & 38.6 & 40.7$\pm$1.3 & $>$100 \\
GSM8K & 7.0$\pm$1.6 & 5.8 & 7.5$\pm$0.1 & 70.6 \\
BoolQ$^\dagger$ & 67.7$\pm$7.5 & 64.5$\pm$11.5 & 73.7 & 34.8 \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
```

脚注：
- $^\ast$: "MMLU $K{=}4$ systematically exceeds E2E across all 3 seeds; attributable to $K{=}4$'s near-uniform per-layer sparsity (std=0.003) matching uniform eval allocation. Independently confirmed: removing the predictor's middle-layer valley yields MMLU=44.0\% (Appendix~\ref{app:constraint})."
- $^\dagger$: "BoolQ exhibits high inter-seed variance for both $K{=}4$ (std=7.5) and $K{=}1$ (std=11.5); gap closure unreliable."

Caption 中删除 "n=1 seed, preliminary"，改为 "3-seed $K{=}4$ mismatch control"。

### 6. Introduction L11 修改

当前："Cross-layer gradient coupling is not the primary driver of PPL-level quality, but contributes meaningfully to downstream task fidelity---a PPL--downstream dissociation quantified in \S\ref{sec:perlayer}."

这句不需要改——它说的是 K=1 发现的定性结论，K=4 数据支持这个方向。保持不变。

## 页数注意

主文 §4.5 长度与之前类似（替换而非增加），Abstract/Conclusion 修改字数相近。Appendix 无限制。编译后确认 8 页。如果溢出，优先精简 §4.5 的数字列举（保留 ARC-c 和 WG 作为代表，GSM8K 移到 appendix reference）。

## 数据

K=4 3-seed downstream:
| Task | K=4 Mean±Std | K=1 Mean | E2E Mean | Genuine Coupling% |
|------|-------------|----------|----------|-------------------|
| PPL | 8.89±0.01 | 9.05 | 8.81 | 33% |
| ARC-c | 40.0±1.3 | 39.9 | 43.7 | 97% |
| WG | 66.9±0.2 | 65.9 | 68.9 | 67% |
| MMLU | 42.7±1.4 | 38.6 | 40.7 | >100% (anomalous) |
| GSM8K | 7.0±1.6 | 5.8 | 7.5 | 29% |
| BoolQ | 67.7±7.5 | 64.5 | 73.7 | 65% (unreliable, std=7.5) |

## 修改清单

### 1. §4.5 L161-163 完全改写（最高优先级）

替换当前 "A $K{=}4$ pilot..." 段落为：

```latex
The $K{=}1$ downstream gap conflates true coupling with train--inference mismatch (training masks 1/32 layers; inference applies all 32). A 3-seed $K{=}4$ control\footnote{Actual sparsity 29.6\%; per-layer sparsity std=0.003 (near-uniform).} decomposes this confound (Appendix~\ref{app:k4_downstream}): \emph{coupling-dominated}---ARC-c retains 97\% of the residual gap (40.0$\pm$1.3 vs.\ E2E 43.7$\pm$2.5) and WG retains 67\% (66.9$\pm$0.2 vs.\ 68.9$\pm$0.4), indicating genuine cross-layer benefits for commonsense reasoning; \emph{mismatch-dominated}---PPL (67\% closure) and GSM8K (71\% closure) gaps largely close; \emph{anomalous}---MMLU exceeds E2E KL across all seeds (42.7$\pm$1.4 vs.\ 40.7$\pm$1.3), attributable to $K{=}4$'s near-uniform learned sparsity matching uniform eval allocation, independently confirmed by a per-layer constraint experiment (Appendix~\ref{app:constraint}).
```

关键改动 vs 当前文本：
- "pilot" → "3-seed control"
- "suggests" → "decomposes/confirms"
- 脚注从 "Single seed (s42)" → "Actual sparsity 29.6%; per-layer sparsity std=0.003"
- BoolQ 从主文移除（方差过大）
- GSM8K 从 "no benefit" 修正为 "mismatch-dominated (71% closure)"
- MMLU 加 constraint 交叉验证

### 2. Abstract L5 修改

当前："coupling scope contributes 21--48\% on downstream tasks; a $K{=}4$ pilot suggests the downstream gap is partly a train--inference mismatch artifact, with task-dependent structure."

改为："coupling scope contributes 10--47\% on downstream tasks (3-seed $K{=}4$-corrected; commonsense 22--47\%, reasoning 10\%); a train--inference mismatch confound in the $K{=}1$ control inflates the $K{=}1$ upper bound (21--48\%)."

### 3. Conclusion L4 修改

当前："cross-layer coupling contributes 21--48\% on downstream tasks, though a $K{=}4$ pilot suggests part of this gap reflects train--inference mismatch (Appendix~\ref{app:k4_downstream})."

改为："cross-layer coupling contributes 10--47\% on downstream tasks (3-seed $K{=}4$-corrected; commonsense 22--47\%, GSM8K 10\%); MMLU anomalously favors $K{=}4$, reflecting allocation alignment confirmed by constraint analysis (Appendices~\ref{app:k4_downstream},~\ref{app:constraint})."

Gap closure $= (K{=}4 - K{=}1) / (\\text{E2E} - K{=}1) \\times 100$.}\n\\label{tab:k4_downstream}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\n& $K{=}4$ & $K{=}1$ & E2E KL & Gap closure (\\%) \\\\\n\\midrule\nPPL$\\downarrow$ & 8.89 & 9.05 & 8.81 & 66.7 \\\\\nARC-c & 41.0 & 39.9 & 43.7 & 28.9 \\\\\nWG & 66.7 & 65.9 & 68.9 & 26.7 \\\\\nMMLU$^\\ast$ & 43.5 & 38.6 & 40.7 & $>$100 \\\\\nGSM8K & 5.2 & 5.8 & 7.5 & $-$35.3 \\\\\nBoolQ$^\\dagger$ & 73.2 & 71.8 & 73.7 & 73.7 \\\\\nPIQA & 74.0 & 75.5 & --- & --- \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\vspace{2pt}\n{\\footnotesize $^\\ast$MMLU $K{=}4$ exceeds E2E; may reflect training--eval alignment.\n$^\\dagger$BoolQ $K{=}1$ uses s42 (3-seed mean$=$64.5$\\pm$11.5, distorted by outlier).}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\section{Per-Layer Sparsity Constraint Analysis}\n\\label{app:constraint}",

```text
155	At 30\%, forward KL (8.81) and JSD (8.90) are near-identical, both outperforming reverse KL (9.26) and dramatically better than MSE (18.52$\pm$1.68; $\sigma$=1.68 vs.\ KL's $\sigma$=0.02, reflecting per-layer signal instability). % exp_id: kl30_ablation_s42, jsd30_ablation_s42, rkl30_ablation_s42, mse30_uniform_s42, mse30_3seed
156	The near-equivalence of KL and JSD suggests the divergence measure matters less than whether the loss optimizes through the output distribution; cross-layer scope provides additional downstream benefits (\S\ref{sec:perlayer}). % exp_id: bce30_uniform_s42, rkl30_downstream_3seed
157	\paragraph{Per-Layer KL: Decomposing Signal Type and Coupling Scope.}
158	\label{sec:perlayer}
159	To isolate cross-layer coupling, we train per-layer KL predictors that mask $K$ random layers per step and compute KL on the full model output, preserving the output-distribution signal without cross-layer gradients.
160	At $K{=}1$, per-layer KL achieves 9.05$\pm$0.02, within 2.7\% of end-to-end KL (8.81$\pm$0.02), while per-layer MSE degrades to 18.52$\pm$1.68 ($2.05\times$ worse). For PPL, signal type\footnote{We use ``signal type'' as shorthand for the training objective's optimization target (output distribution vs.\ intermediate activations); this dimension also entails differences in gradient flow topology and optimization landscape that are not independently controlled.} accounts for up to 97.5\% of the gap (upper bound; signal type and coupling scope are not fully orthogonal).
161	The $K{=}1$ downstream gap conflates true coupling with train--inference mismatch (training masks 1/32 layers; inference applies all 32). A $K{=}4$ pilot\footnote{Single seed (s42); actual sparsity 29.6\% vs.\ target 30\%.} suggests this confound is substantial and reveals task-dependent structure (Appendix~\ref{app:k4_downstream}): \emph{mismatch-dominated}---PPL (67\%) and BoolQ (73\%) gaps largely close with increased $K$; \emph{coupling-dominated}---ARC-c and WG retain substantial residual gap (60--73\% depending on $K{=}1$ baseline), indicating genuine cross-layer benefits; \emph{anomalous}---MMLU exceeds E2E KL (43.5 vs.\ 40.7$\pm$1.3, possibly training--eval alignment), while GSM8K and PIQA show no benefit. % exp_id: perlayer_kl_30_3seed, perlayer_kl_downstream, mse30_downstream_decomp, perlayer_kl_k4_30
162	At 50\% ($K{=}1$ only), the gap widens sharply: per-layer KL degrades to 34.85 vs.\ 11.92 for E2E KL (+192\%), with coupling's share rising from 2.5\% to 10.6\% ($4\times$; Table~\ref{tab:ablation}) and downstream falling to TEAL level. % exp_id: perlayer_kl_downstream, perlayer_kl_50_s42
163	
164	\paragraph{STE Failure and Loss--Relaxation Interaction.}
165	\label{sec:ablation_ste}
166	At 30\%, STE yields only 20.8\% effective sparsity (vs.\ target 30\%), confirming Gumbel-Sigmoid is necessary. BCE+Gumbel (32.22) underperforms standard BCE (27.97); the loss effect ($\Delta$PPL=20.4) is $5.1\times$ the relaxation effect ($\Delta$PPL=3.98). % exp_id: ste_30pct_sparsity_failure, bce_gumbel_no_penalty_s42, bce_gumbel_lagrangian_isolation, eval_bce_v3_s42, bce_ste_s42
167	
168	\paragraph{Compensation Redundancy.}
169	\label{sec:compensation}
```

### Tool result

_source line 9216 · 2026-05-22T16:52:51.535Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01658h3SQ2qBZi8hPUtm5bwn`</summary>

```text
1	Learned sparsity predictors for SwiGLU LLMs predominantly use binary cross-entropy (BCE), training each neuron independently, yet inference allocates budgets globally via top-$k$ ranking.
2	We train lightweight predictors ($<$1\% of base parameters) with forward KL divergence, coupling all mask decisions through the output distribution.
3	On LLaMA-3.1-8B at 30\% sparsity, the KL predictor reduces perplexity by 14.5\% over TEAL at matched sparsity, with commonsense gains but knowledge-intensive decline; post-hoc compensation (+67.2M params) becomes redundant.
4	Output-distribution optimization yields masks that diverge 46--68\% from magnitude-based targets; a target-swap ablation suggests these discovered patterns---not the training mechanism itself---as the proximate quality driver.
5	An output-distribution alignment ranking emerges across five loss configurations; a per-layer KL control decomposes this into two dimensions: signal type dominates PPL (up to 97.5\% of the gap at 30\%; upper bound, as dimensions are not fully orthogonal), while coupling scope contributes 21--48\% on downstream tasks; a $K{=}4$ pilot suggests the downstream gap is partly a train--inference mismatch artifact, with task-dependent structure.
6	Cross-architecture evaluation on Mistral-7B-v0.3 and Qwen-2.5-14B confirms PPL generalization with scale-dependent downstream effects.
7	This is primarily an empirical study; wall-clock speedup requires sparse kernel support not addressed here.
8	
```

```text
1	\section{Conclusion}
2	\label{sec:conclusion}
3	
4	Output-distribution alignment is the dominant design dimension for PPL (signal type: up to 97.5\% of the gap at 30\%; upper bound, not fully orthogonal); cross-layer coupling contributes 21--48\% on downstream tasks, though a $K{=}4$ pilot suggests part of this gap reflects train--inference mismatch (Appendix~\ref{app:k4_downstream}).
5	A predictor ($<$1\% of base) reduces TEAL's PPL by 14.5\% via masks 46--68\% divergent from magnitude; KL outperforms TEAL on commonsense (ARC-c, WG, BoolQ) but underperforms on knowledge tasks (MMLU, GSM8K) across tested scales---a learned allocation trade-off, not an inherent objective limitation (Appendix~\ref{app:constraint}).
6
```

```text
20	Our contributions are:
21	\begin{itemize}[nosep,leftmargin=*]
22	\item An output-distribution alignment ranking across five losses, decomposed by a per-layer KL control into signal type (up to 97.5\% of PPL gap; upper bound, not fully orthogonal) and coupling scope, with a $K{=}4$ mismatch control revealing task-dependent downstream structure (\S\ref{sec:perlayer});
23	\item Output-distribution losses outperform intermediate losses regardless of coupling scope, with exploratory cross-scale evidence at 7--14B (\S\ref{sec:experiments}).
24	\item A target-swap ablation suggests that KL's discovered mask targets drive inference-stage quality; cross-layer coupling refines downstream fidelity beyond what PPL captures (\S\ref{sec:perlayer}).
25	\item These mask patterns render post-hoc compensation (+67.2M params) redundant at both tested sparsity levels (\S\ref{sec:compensation}).
26	\end{itemize}
```

```json
  "new_string": "The $K{=}1$ downstream gap conflates true coupling with train--inference mismatch (training masks 1/32 layers; inference applies all 32). A 3-seed $K{=}4$ control\\footnote{Actual sparsity 29.6\\%; per-layer sparsity std=0.003 (near-uniform).} decomposes this confound (Appendix~\\ref{app:k4_downstream}): \\emph{coupling-dominated}---ARC-c retains 97\\% of the residual gap (40.0$\\pm$1.3 vs.\\ E2E 43.7$\\pm$2.5) and WG retains 67\\% (66.9$\\pm$0.2 vs.\\ 68.9$\\pm$0.4), indicating genuine cross-layer benefits for commonsense reasoning; \\emph{mismatch-dominated}---PPL (67\\% closure) and GSM8K (71\\% closure) gaps largely close; \\emph{anomalous}---MMLU exceeds E2E KL across all seeds (42.7$\\pm$1.4 vs.\\ 40.7$\\pm$1.3), attributable to $K{=}4$'s near-uniform learned sparsity matching uniform eval allocation, independently confirmed by a per-layer constraint experiment (Appendix~\\ref{app:constraint}). % exp_id: perlayer_kl_30_3seed, perlayer_kl_downstream, mse30_downstream_decomp, perlayer_kl_k4_3seed"
```

```json
    "newString": "while coupling scope contributes 10--47\\% on downstream tasks (3-seed $K{=}4$-corrected; commonsense 22--47\\%, reasoning 10\\%); a train--inference mismatch confound in the $K{=}1$ control inflates the $K{=}1$ upper bound (21--48\\%).",
    "originalFile": "Learned sparsity predictors for SwiGLU LLMs predominantly use binary cross-entropy (BCE), training each neuron independently, yet inference allocates budgets globally via top-$k$ ranking.\nWe train lightweight predictors ($<$1\\% of base parameters) with forward KL divergence, coupling all mask decisions through the output distribution.\nOn LLaMA-3.1-8B at 30\\% sparsity, the KL predictor reduces perplexity by 14.5\\% over TEAL at matched sparsity, with commonsense gains but knowledge-intensive decline; post-hoc compensation (+67.2M params) becomes redundant.\nOutput-distribution optimization yields masks that diverge 46--68\\% from magnitude-based targets; a target-swap ablation suggests these discovered patterns---not the training mechanism itself---as the proximate quality driver.\nAn output-distribution alignment ranking emerges across five loss configurations; a per-layer KL control decomposes this into two dimensions: signal type dominates PPL (up to 97.5\\% of the gap at 30\\%; upper bound, as dimensions are not fully orthogonal), while coupling scope contributes 21--48\\% on downstream tasks; a $K{=}4$ pilot suggests the downstream gap is partly a train--inference mismatch artifact, with task-dependent structure.\nCross-architecture evaluation on Mistral-7B-v0.3 and Qwen-2.5-14B confirms PPL generalization with scale-dependent downstream effects.\nThis is primarily an empirical study; wall-clock speedup requires sparse kernel support not addressed here.\n",
    "structuredPatch": [

{
```

```json
  "parentUuid": "121217d9-58eb-4948-a0e4-f9f8fccd3da8",

    "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_14/projects/distill_sparse_swiglu/paper/introduction.tex",
    "oldString": "and coupling scope, with a $K{=}4$ mismatch control revealing task-dependent downstream structure",
    "newString": "and coupling scope (10--47\\% on downstream tasks, $K{=}4$-corrected), with task-dependent downstream structure",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\nHow the training objective shapes learned sparsity prediction quality is an open question for SwiGLU LLMs~\\cite{shazeer2020glu}. SwiGLU FFN blocks exhibit high contextual sparsity, but their gated activation makes importance prediction challenging: neuron importance depends on both gate and up-projection values, a target that magnitude-based methods handle suboptimally.\nLearned sparsity predictors~\\cite{liu2023dejavu,gautam2026fastforward} exploit this property by training small networks to anticipate which neurons are important, but they predominantly rely on binary cross-entropy (BCE) loss, treating each neuron as an independent classification target.\nYet at inference, sparsity budgets are allocated globally: the TEAL (training-free activation sparsity;~\\citealp{liu2025teal}) top-$k$ mechanism ranks all neurons across all layers and retains only those above a model-wide threshold.\nThis mismatch between per-neuron training and global inference allocation has not been formally analyzed.\nWhile MaskLLM~\\cite{fang2024maskllm} applies KL-based mask optimization, it targets static N:M weight sparsity; activation sparsity requires masks that generalize to unseen inputs, vary per token, and coordinate across layers through global allocation.\n\nGradient analysis reveals a structural asymmetry: BCE's gradient $\\partial \\mathcal{L}_{\\text{BCE}} / \\partial s_j = \\sigma(s_j) - m_j^*$ depends only on neuron $j$, while under KL divergence, the gradient couples each neuron to all others through the downstream Jacobian (\\S\\ref{sec:method:gradient}).\nKL's coupled gradients discover neuron importance patterns that diverge 68\\% from magnitude-based masks (per-layer Jaccard 32\\%).\nA target-swap ablation suggests that these discovered mask targets are the proximate quality driver at inference: BCE trained on KL-derived targets reproduces KL quality (max $\\Delta$$\\leq$0.79pp at 50\\%, s42), while magnitude-trained BCE achieves 28.13$\\pm$0.20. Cross-layer gradient coupling is not the primary driver of PPL-level quality, but contributes meaningfully to downstream task fidelity---a PPL--downstream dissociation quantified in \\S\\ref{sec:perlayer}.\n\nWe instantiate a lightweight predictor (75.9M params, $<$1\\% of base) trained with forward KL and Gumbel-Sigmoid relaxation~\\cite{jang2017gumbel} on LLaMA-3.1-8B~\\cite{grattafiori2024llama3}.\nAt 30\\% sparsity under uniform allocation, the predictor reduces TEAL's perplexity by 14.5\\% at matched sparsity (8.81 vs.\\ 10.30 at 28.5\\%) with stable downstream (MMLU 40.7$\\pm$1.3\\%, 3-seed); global top-$k$ amplifies cross-seed variance ($7.3\\times$).\nAt 50\\%, the perplexity advantage widens (50\\% over TEAL), but downstream reasoning collapses regardless of method.\n\nCross-architecture evaluation on Mistral-7B-v0.3~\\cite{jiang2023mistral} (3-seed) confirms the PPL advantage ($-$17.3\\%) with robust commonsense gains but tied MMLU; preliminary evaluation on Qwen-2.5-14B~\\cite{qwen2024qwen25} shows consistent PPL improvement but reasoning decline (GSM8K $-$20pp, MMLU $-$2.8pp).\nThis paper is primarily an empirical study: we do not propose a new architecture or algorithm, but rather characterize how the training objective shapes sparsity prediction quality, and analyze interactions between allocation strategy, sparsity level, and model scale. The empirical finding---that output-distribution alignment is the critical design dimension for learned sparsity---is the paper's central contribution. Wall-clock speedup requires sparse kernel support; we do not address latency optimization (Appendix~\\ref{app:latency}).\n\nOur contributions are:\n\\begin{itemize}[nosep,leftmargin=*]\n\\item An output-distribution alignment ranking across five losses, decomposed by a per-layer KL control into signal type (up to 97.5\\% of PPL gap; upper bound, not fully orthogonal) and coupling scope, with a $K{=}4$ mismatch control revealing task-dependent downstream structure (\\S\\ref{sec:perlayer});\n\\item Output-distribution losses outperform intermediate losses regardless of coupling scope, with exploratory cross-scale evidence at 7--14B (\\S\\ref{sec:experiments}).\n\\item A target-swap ablation suggests that KL's discovered mask targets drive inference-stage quality; cross-layer coupling refines downstream fidelity beyond what PPL captures (\\S\\ref{sec:perlayer}).\n\\item These mask patterns render post-hoc compensation (+67.2M params) redundant at both tested sparsity levels (\\S\\ref{sec:compensation}).\n\\end{itemize}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig1_overview.pdf}\n\\caption{Method overview: a lightweight per-layer predictor produces importance scores trained via KL divergence with Gumbel-Sigmoid relaxation. At inference, scores feed TEAL's global top-$k$ allocation without compensation networks.}\n\\label{fig:overview}\n\\end{figure}\n",
    "structuredPatch": [
      {

        "oldLines": 7,

          "+\\item An output-distribution alignment ranking across five losses, decomposed by a per-layer KL control into signal type (up to 97.5\\% of PPL gap; upper bound, not fully orthogonal) and coupling scope (10--47\\% on downstream tasks, $K{=}4$-corrected), with task-dependent downstream structure (\\S\\ref{sec:perlayer});",
          " \\item Output-distribution losses outperform intermediate losses regardless of coupling scope, with exploratory cross-scale evidence at 7--14B (\\S\\ref{sec:experiments}).",
```

```json
    "oldString": "A 3-seed $K{=}4$ control\\footnote{Actual sparsity 29.6\\%; per-layer sparsity std=0.003 (near-uniform).} decomposes this confound (Appendix~\\ref{app:k4_downstream}): \\emph{coupling-dominated}---ARC-c retains 97\\% of the residual gap (40.0$\\pm$1.3 vs.\\ E2E 43.7$\\pm$2.5) and WG retains 67\\% (66.9$\\pm$0.2 vs.\\ 68.9$\\pm$0.4), indicating genuine cross-layer benefits for commonsense reasoning; \\emph{mismatch-dominated}---PPL (67\\% closure) and GSM8K (71\\% closure) gaps largely close; \\emph{anomalous}---MMLU exceeds E2E KL across all seeds (42.7$\\pm$1.4 vs.\\ 40.7$\\pm$1.3), attributable to $K{=}4$'s near-uniform learned sparsity matching uniform eval allocation, independently confirmed by a per-layer constraint experiment (Appendix~\\ref{app:constraint}).",
    "newString": "A 3-seed $K{=}4$ control\\footnote{Actual sparsity 29.6\\%; per-layer sparsity std=0.003 (near-uniform).} decomposes this confound (Appendix~\\ref{app:k4_downstream}): \\emph{coupling-dominated}---ARC-c (97\\% residual gap) and WG (67\\%) indicate genuine cross-layer benefits for commonsense; \\emph{mismatch-dominated}---PPL (67\\% closure) and GSM8K (71\\%) gaps largely close; \\emph{anomalous}---MMLU exceeds E2E across all seeds (42.7$\\pm$1.4 vs.\\ 40.7$\\pm$1.3), explained by $K{=}4$'s near-uniform allocation matching uniform inference (Appendix~\\ref{app:constraint}).",
    "originalFile": null,

      {
        "oldStart": 158,

        "lines": [
          " \\label{sec:perlayer}",
          " To isolate cross-layer coupling, we train per-layer KL predictors that mask $K$ random layers per step and compute KL on the full model output, preserving the output-distribution signal without cross-layer gradients.",
          " At $K{=}1$, per-layer KL achieves 9.05$\\pm$0.02, within 2.7\\% of end-to-end KL (8.81$\\pm$0.02), while per-layer MSE degrades to 18.52$\\pm$1.68 ($2.05\\times$ worse). For PPL, signal type\\footnote{We use ``signal type'' as shorthand for the training objective's optimization target (output distribution vs.\\ intermediate activations); this dimension also entails differences in gradient flow topology and optimization landscape that are not independently controlled.} accounts for up to 97.5\\% of the gap (upper bound; signal type and coupling scope are not fully orthogonal).",
          "-The $K{=}1$ downstream gap conflates true coupling with train--inference mismatch (training masks 1/32 layers; inference applies all 32). A 3-seed $K{=}4$ control\\footnote{Actual sparsity 29.6\\%; per-layer sparsity std=0.003 (near-uniform).} decomposes this confound (Appendix~\\ref{app:k4_downstream}): \\emph{coupling-dominated}---ARC-c retains 97\\% of the residual gap (40.0$\\pm$1.3 vs.\\ E2E 43.7$\\pm$2.5) and WG retains 67\\% (66.9$\\pm$0.2 vs.\\ 68.9$\\pm$0.4), indicating genuine cross-layer benefits for commonsense reasoning; \\emph{mismatch-dominated}---PPL (67\\% closure) and GSM8K (71\\% closure) gaps largely close; \\emph{anomalous}---MMLU exceeds E2E KL across all seeds (42.7$\\pm$1.4 vs.\\ 40.7$\\pm$1.3), attributable to $K{=}4$'s near-uniform learned sparsity matching uniform eval allocation, independently confirmed by a per-layer constraint experiment (Appendix~\\ref{app:constraint}). % exp_id: perlayer_kl_30_3seed, perlayer_kl_downstream, mse30_downstream_decomp, perlayer_kl_k4_3seed",
          "+The $K{=}1$ downstream gap conflates true coupling with train--inference mismatch (training masks 1/32 layers; inference applies all 32). A 3-seed $K{=}4$ control\\footnote{Actual sparsity 29.6\\%; per-layer sparsity std=0.003 (near-uniform).} decomposes this confound (Appendix~\\ref{app:k4_downstream}): \\emph{coupling-dominated}---ARC-c (97\\% residual gap) and WG (67\\%) indicate genuine cross-layer benefits for commonsense; \\emph{mismatch-dominated}---PPL (67\\% closure) and GSM8K (71\\%) gaps largely close; \\emph{anomalous}---MMLU exceeds E2E across all seeds (42.7$\\pm$1.4 vs.\\ 40.7$\\pm$1.3), explained by $K{=}4$'s near-uniform allocation matching uniform inference (Appendix~\\ref{app:constraint}). % exp_id: perlayer_kl_30_3seed, perlayer_kl_downstream, mse30_downstream_decomp, perlayer_kl_k4_3seed",
          " At 50\\% ($K{=}1$ only), the gap widens sharply: per-layer KL degrades to 34.85 vs.\\ 11.92 for E2E KL (+192\\%), with coupling's share rising from 2.5\\% to 10.6\\% ($4\\times$; Table~\\ref{tab:ablation}) and downstream falling to TEAL level. % exp_id: perlayer_kl_downstream, perlayer_kl_50_s42",
          " ",
          " \\paragraph{STE Failure and Loss--Relaxation Interaction.}"
        ]
      }
    ],
```

```text
526	Table~\ref{tab:k4_downstream} reports 3-seed $K{=}4$ per-layer KL results at 30\% sparsity.
527	Training with $K{=}4$ random layers per step reduces the train--inference mismatch (4/32 vs.\ 1/32 layers masked during training, while inference applies all 32).
528	The gap closure column reports the fraction of the $K{=}1 \to$ E2E KL gap closed by $K{=}4$.
529	
530	\begin{table}[h]
531	\centering
532	\caption{3-seed $K{=}4$ mismatch control at 30\% sparsity (LLaMA-3.1-8B, uniform allocation, $s \in \{42, 123, 456\}$). Actual sparsity 29.6\%; per-layer sparsity std=0.003 (near-uniform). Gap closure $= (K{=}4 - K{=}1) / (\text{E2E} - K{=}1) \times 100$.}
533	\label{tab:k4_downstream}
534	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{
535	\begin{tabular}{lcccc}
536	\toprule

538	\midrule
539	PPL$\downarrow$ & 8.89$\pm$0.01 & 9.05$\pm$0.02 & 8.81$\pm$0.02 & 66.7 \\
540	ARC-c & 40.0$\pm$1.3 & 39.9 & 43.7$\pm$2.5 & 2.6 \\
541	WG & 66.9$\pm$0.2 & 65.9 & 68.9$\pm$0.4 & 33.3 \\
542	MMLU$^\ast$ & 42.7$\pm$1.4 & 38.6 & 40.7$\pm$1.3 & $>$100 \\
543	GSM8K & 7.0$\pm$1.6 & 5.8 & 7.5$\pm$0.1 & 70.6 \\
544	BoolQ$^\dagger$ & 67.7$\pm$7.5 & 64.5$\pm$11.5 & 73.7 & 34.8 \\
545	\bottomrule
546	\end{tabular}}
547	\vspace{2pt}
548	{\footnotesize $^\ast$MMLU $K{=}4$ systematically exceeds E2E across all 3 seeds; attributable to $K{=}4$'s near-uniform per-layer sparsity (std=0.003) matching uniform eval allocation. Independently confirmed: removing the predictor's middle-layer valley yields MMLU=44.0\% (Appendix~\ref{app:constraint}).
549	$^\dagger$BoolQ exhibits high inter-seed variance for both $K{=}4$ (std=7.5) and $K{=}1$ (std=11.5); gap closure unreliable.}
550	\end{table}

554
```

```text
20	Our contributions are:
21	\begin{itemize}[nosep,leftmargin=*]
22	\item An output-distribution alignment ranking across five losses, decomposed by a per-layer KL control into signal type (up to 97.5\% of PPL gap; upper bound, not fully orthogonal) and coupling scope (10--47\% on downstream tasks, $K{=}4$-corrected), with task-dependent downstream structure (\S\ref{sec:perlayer});
23	\item Output-distribution losses outperform intermediate losses regardless of coupling scope, with exploratory cross-scale evidence at 7--14B (\S\ref{sec:experiments}).
24	\item A target-swap ablation suggests that KL's discovered mask targets drive inference-stage quality; cross-layer coupling refines downstream fidelity beyond what PPL captures (\S\ref{sec:perlayer}).
25	\item These mask patterns render post-hoc compensation (+67.2M params) redundant at both tested sparsity levels (\S\ref{sec:compensation}).
26	\end{itemize}
27	
```

```text
1	\section{Related Work}
2	
3	\paragraph{Training-Free Activation Sparsity.}
4	TEAL~\cite{liu2025teal} applies global top-$k$ selection by activation magnitude and achieves 40--50\% sparsity with no training cost.
5	CATS~\cite{lee2024cats} adapts per-layer thresholds for SiLU/SwiGLU activations; R-Sparse~\cite{zhang2025rsparse} predicts sparsity from rank-aware SVD projections; DIP~\cite{federici2025dip} combines dynamic input pruning with cache-aware masking; SparseInfer~\cite{shin2025sparseinfer} predicts activation sparsity patterns from attention outputs without additional training.
6	Wang et al.~\cite{wang2025dynamicsparse} address dynamic sparsity--quantization interaction; AWQ~\cite{lin2024awq} applies activation-aware weight quantization for on-device deployment; Outlier Suppression+~\cite{wei2023outlier} mitigates outlier-induced quantization errors via shifting and scaling.
7	Dhar et al.~\cite{dhar2024activation} characterize activation sparsity opportunities across architectures, showing that SwiGLU models exhibit lower natural sparsity than ReLU counterparts.
8	OWL~\cite{yin2024owl} determines non-uniform per-layer sparsity ratios based on activation outlier distribution for weight pruning.
9	All training-free methods determine masks from fixed statistics or input-independent criteria, while our predictor generates \emph{input-dependent} masks trained to minimize output-distribution divergence.
10	
```

```json
    "oldString": "\\item An output-distribution alignment ranking across five losses, decomposed by a per-layer KL control into signal type (up to 97.5\\% of PPL gap; upper bound, not fully orthogonal) and coupling scope (10--47\\% on downstream tasks, $K{=}4$-corrected), with task-dependent downstream structure (\\S\\ref{sec:perlayer});\n\\item Output-distribution losses outperform intermediate losses regardless of coupling scope, with exploratory cross-scale evidence at 7--14B (\\S\\ref{sec:experiments}).",
    "newString": "\\item An output-distribution alignment ranking across five losses, decomposed by a per-layer KL control into signal type (up to 97.5\\% of PPL gap; upper bound, not fully orthogonal) and coupling scope (10--47\\% on downstream tasks, $K{=}4$-corrected), with task-dependent downstream structure (\\S\\ref{sec:perlayer});\n\\item Exploratory cross-scale evidence at 7--14B confirming output-distribution losses outperform intermediate losses regardless of coupling scope (\\S\\ref{sec:experiments}).",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\nHow the training objective shapes learned sparsity prediction quality is an open question for SwiGLU LLMs~\\cite{shazeer2020glu}. SwiGLU FFN blocks exhibit high contextual sparsity, but their gated activation makes importance prediction challenging: neuron importance depends on both gate and up-projection values, a target that magnitude-based methods handle suboptimally.\nLearned sparsity predictors~\\cite{liu2023dejavu,gautam2026fastforward} exploit this property by training small networks to anticipate which neurons are important, but they predominantly rely on binary cross-entropy (BCE) loss, treating each neuron as an independent classification target.\nYet at inference, sparsity budgets are allocated globally: the TEAL (training-free activation sparsity;~\\citealp{liu2025teal}) top-$k$ mechanism ranks all neurons across all layers and retains only those above a model-wide threshold.\nThis mismatch between per-neuron training and global inference allocation has not been formally analyzed.\nWhile MaskLLM~\\cite{fang2024maskllm} applies KL-based mask optimization, it targets static N:M weight sparsity; activation sparsity requires masks that generalize to unseen inputs, vary per token, and coordinate across layers through global allocation.\n\nGradient analysis reveals a structural asymmetry: BCE's gradient $\\partial \\mathcal{L}_{\\text{BCE}} / \\partial s_j = \\sigma(s_j) - m_j^*$ depends only on neuron $j$, while under KL divergence, the gradient couples each neuron to all others through the downstream Jacobian (\\S\\ref{sec:method:gradient}).\nKL's coupled gradients discover neuron importance patterns that diverge 68\\% from magnitude-based masks (per-layer Jaccard 32\\%).\nA target-swap ablation suggests that these discovered mask targets are the proximate quality driver at inference: BCE trained on KL-derived targets reproduces KL quality (max $\\Delta$$\\leq$0.79pp at 50\\%, s42), while magnitude-trained BCE achieves 28.13$\\pm$0.20. Cross-layer gradient coupling is not the primary driver of PPL-level quality, but contributes meaningfully to downstream task fidelity---a PPL--downstream dissociation quantified in \\S\\ref{sec:perlayer}.\n\nWe instantiate a lightweight predictor (75.9M params, $<$1\\% of base) trained with forward KL and Gumbel-Sigmoid relaxation~\\cite{jang2017gumbel} on LLaMA-3.1-8B~\\cite{grattafiori2024llama3}.\nAt 30\\% sparsity under uniform allocation, the predictor reduces TEAL's perplexity by 14.5\\% at matched sparsity (8.81 vs.\\ 10.30 at 28.5\\%) with stable downstream (MMLU 40.7$\\pm$1.3\\%, 3-seed); global top-$k$ amplifies cross-seed variance ($7.3\\times$).\nAt 50\\%, the perplexity advantage widens (50\\% over TEAL), but downstream reasoning collapses regardless of method.\n\nCross-architecture evaluation on Mistral-7B-v0.3~\\cite{jiang2023mistral} (3-seed) confirms the PPL advantage ($-$17.3\\%) with robust commonsense gains but tied MMLU; preliminary evaluation on Qwen-2.5-14B~\\cite{qwen2024qwen25} shows consistent PPL improvement but reasoning decline (GSM8K $-$20pp, MMLU $-$2.8pp).\nThis paper is primarily an empirical study: we do not propose a new architecture or algorithm, but rather characterize how the training objective shapes sparsity prediction quality, and analyze interactions between allocation strategy, sparsity level, and model scale. The empirical finding---that output-distribution alignment is the critical design dimension for learned sparsity---is the paper's central contribution. Wall-clock speedup requires sparse kernel support; we do not address latency optimization (Appendix~\\ref{app:latency}).\n\nOur contributions are:\n\\begin{itemize}[nosep,leftmargin=*]\n\\item An output-distribution alignment ranking across five losses, decomposed by a per-layer KL control into signal type (up to 97.5\\% of PPL gap; upper bound, not fully orthogonal) and coupling scope (10--47\\% on downstream tasks, $K{=}4$-corrected), with task-dependent downstream structure (\\S\\ref{sec:perlayer});\n\\item Output-distribution losses outperform intermediate losses regardless of coupling scope, with exploratory cross-scale evidence at 7--14B (\\S\\ref{sec:experiments}).\n\\item A target-swap ablation suggests that KL's discovered mask targets drive inference-stage quality; cross-layer coupling refines downstream fidelity beyond what PPL captures (\\S\\ref{sec:perlayer}).\n\\item These mask patterns render post-hoc compensation (+67.2M params) redundant at both tested sparsity levels (\\S\\ref{sec:compensation}).\n\\end{itemize}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig1_overview.pdf}\n\\caption{Method overview: a lightweight per-layer predictor produces importance scores trained via KL divergence with Gumbel-Sigmoid relaxation. At inference, scores feed TEAL's global top-$k$ allocation without compensation networks.}\n\\label{fig:overview}\n\\end{figure}\n",
    "structuredPatch": [
      {
        "oldStart": 20,
        "oldLines": 7,
        "newStart": 20,
        "newLines": 7,
        "lines": [
          " Our contributions are:",
          " \\begin{itemize}[nosep,leftmargin=*]",
          " \\item An output-distribution alignment ranking across five losses, decomposed by a per-layer KL control into signal type (up to 97.5\\% of PPL gap; upper bound, not fully orthogonal) and coupling scope (10--47\\% on downstream tasks, $K{=}4$-corrected), with task-dependent downstream structure (\\S\\ref{sec:perlayer});",
          "-\\item Output-distribution losses outperform intermediate losses regardless of coupling scope, with exploratory cross-scale evidence at 7--14B (\\S\\ref{sec:experiments}).",
```

[Director 指令] [Patch] R22 审稿写作修复，Tier 1 优先。DDL 5/25。不用验证引用。

## Tier 1 修复（必做）

**1. N1 — C1 因果声明降级为 transferability**

Abstract 当前: "these discovered patterns—not the training mechanism itself—as the proximate quality driver"
→ 改为: "these discovered patterns carry most of the quality information: BCE trained on KL-derived targets approaches KL-level PPL (max $\Delta$$\leq$0.79pp, 3-seed)"

Introduction L11 当前: "A target-swap ablation suggests that these discovered mask targets are the proximate quality driver at inference"
→ 改为: "A target-swap ablation suggests that KL-discovered mask targets carry most of the quality information at inference"

§4.4 (target-swap section): 删除所有排除性声明（"not the training mechanism itself"）。加一句: "while the causal role of cross-layer coupling during inference (vs.\ training-time discovery) remains an open question"。

§4.4 加 s42 oracle 细节: "For s42, the oracle source is an earlier checkpoint (2000 steps); s123/s456 use seed-matched fully converged checkpoints, yielding tighter transfer (gap $\leq$0.03pp vs.\ 0.65pp for s42)."

**2. N2 — 97.5% 从精确数字改为定性**

Abstract: "signal type dominates PPL (up to 97.5\% of the gap at 30\%; upper bound, as dimensions are not fully orthogonal)" 
→ 改为: "signal type accounts for the majority of the PPL gap (upper bound; K=1 control confounded by train-inference mismatch)"

Introduction contribution #1: 同步修改，去掉 "97.5%"，改为 "signal type dominates PPL (upper bound)"

Conclusion: 同步去掉 "97.5%"

§4.5: 将 Footnote 5 的 orthogonality caveat ("not fully orthogonal") 提升到正文段落中。在 "signal type accounts for up to 97.5%" 后面紧跟: "This is an upper bound: the K=1 control trains on 1/32 layers per step but infers on all 32, conflating coupling removal with train-inference mismatch. A K=4 control (\S\ref{app:k4_downstream}) partially deconfounds this."

**3. N3 — §4.2 Task-Type Split 深化分析**

当前 §4.2 已有 constraint 数据。加强：
- 在 constraint 数据后加: "This reveals a task-dependent allocation trade-off, not a failure mode: the KL predictor learns to protect layers critical for commonsense reasoning at the cost of knowledge-intensive pathways. Task-aware allocation—assigning different per-layer budgets for different task families—is a promising direction to resolve this trade-off."
- 加 Geva et al. 2023 引用 (已在 bib 中): "consistent with findings that factual associations concentrate in specific FFN layers~\cite{geva2023dissecting}"

**4. W7 — Abstract 第5句简化**

当前 (~60 词嵌套句): "An output-distribution alignment ranking emerges across five loss configurations; a per-layer KL control decomposes this into two dimensions: signal type dominates PPL (up to 97.5%...); coupling scope contributes 10--47%..."
→ 拆为两句: 
"Signal type (output-distribution vs.\ intermediate loss) accounts for the majority of the PPL gap at 30\% (upper bound; not fully orthogonal with coupling scope). Cross-layer coupling contributes 10--47\% on downstream tasks (3-seed $K{=}4$-corrected; commonsense 22--47\%, reasoning 10\%)."

**5. W8 — Conclusion BoolQ 替换 + 加 limitations**

删除 Conclusion 中的 BoolQ 引用（variance 太高）。如果 Conclusion 引用了 commonsense tasks，用 HellaSwag/PIQA 替换。

Conclusion 末尾加一句: "Limitations include MMLU/GSM8K regression across all tested scales, absence of wall-clock speedup, and evaluation bounded at 7--14B."

## Tier 2 修复（应做，页数允许时）

**6. W1 — §1 speedup 条件**
在 wall-clock disclaimer 句后加: "Realizing wall-clock gains requires sparse GEMM kernels that exploit the predicted masks."

**7. W3 — §4.1 matched sparsity 显著化**
把 "14.5% at matched 28.5% sparsity" 从括号中提出：改写为主句 "At matched 28.5\% sparsity, the improvement is 14.5\% (Appendix~\ref{app:ood})."

**8. M2 — §3.5 ε=0.02 justification**
加: "chosen to accommodate $\pm$2\% sparsity estimation noise from Gumbel sampling"

**9. M3 — §4.3 wall-clock 解释**
加: "(KL's per-step cost includes a dense forward pass absent in BCE, offsetting the 2.7$\times$ step difference)"

**10. M4 — "Adaptive Penalty Controller" 重命名**
→ "Geometric Penalty Schedule"

**11. K=4 GSM8K per-seed instability footnote**
§4.5 或 Appendix K=4 表加 footnote: "Individual-seed gap closure varies widely for GSM8K due to high sampling noise at 1319 examples."

**12. M6 — Limitations 加 sequence length**
加: "Predictor mask quality under long-context ($>$4096 tokens) remains untested."

```text
30	
31	\paragraph{Task-Type Split.}
32	KL outperforms TEAL on commonsense reasoning (ARC-c, WG, HellaSwag, PIQA: 4/4 wins at 30\% uniform) but underperforms on knowledge-intensive tasks (MMLU, GSM8K: 0/2).
33	The KL predictor protects L14--L18 (14--18\% sparsity vs.\ 32\% global mean) while aggressively pruning L22--L28 (48--57\%). Per-layer constraint interventions (Appendix~\ref{app:constraint}) confirm this is a task-dependent trade-off: removing the valley improves MMLU +3.3pp and GSM8K +1.5pp but reduces ARC-c by 4.4pp. % exp_id: bench_kl30_uniform, teal30_uniform, hellaswag_piqa_kl30_uniform, perlayer_sparsity_analysis, constraint_protect_late, constraint_remove_valley
34	
35	At 50\% sparsity, the PPL advantage widens: 11.92 vs.\ 23.90 (50\% reduction over TEAL). % exp_id: bench_kl50_uniform, teal50_uniform
36	The predictor generalizes to C4 validation at both 30\% (PPL 16.03$\pm$0.01, 2-seed, global allocation) and 50\% (52.5\% over TEAL; Appendix~\ref{app:ood}).
37	However, all methods exhibit downstream collapse on MMLU (28.1$\pm$0.2\% vs.\ 28.0$\pm$0.6\%, both $\approx$ random, 3-seed) and GSM8K ($<$2.2\%); this is a capacity-limited regime where no allocation strategy can recover. % exp_id: bench_kl50_global, d030_kl50_uniform, multi_seed_kl50_global
38	
39	\begin{table}[t]
```

### Tool result

_source line 9380 · 2026-05-22T17:40:11.796Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01LJwYTEbGa3BrPCJ2JovEoh`</summary>

```text
1	Learned sparsity predictors for SwiGLU LLMs predominantly use binary cross-entropy (BCE), training each neuron independently, yet inference allocates budgets globally via top-$k$ ranking.
2	We train lightweight predictors ($<$1\% of base parameters) with forward KL divergence, coupling all mask decisions through the output distribution.
3	On LLaMA-3.1-8B at 30\% sparsity, the KL predictor reduces perplexity by 14.5\% over TEAL at matched sparsity, with commonsense gains but knowledge-intensive decline; post-hoc compensation (+67.2M params) becomes redundant.
4	Output-distribution optimization yields masks that diverge 46--68\% from magnitude-based targets; a target-swap ablation suggests these discovered patterns---not the training mechanism itself---as the proximate quality driver.
5	An output-distribution alignment ranking emerges across five loss configurations; a per-layer KL control decomposes this into two dimensions: signal type dominates PPL (up to 97.5\% of the gap at 30\%; upper bound, as dimensions are not fully orthogonal), while coupling scope contributes 10--47\% on downstream tasks (3-seed $K{=}4$-corrected; commonsense 22--47\%, reasoning 10\%); a train--inference mismatch confound in the $K{=}1$ control inflates the $K{=}1$ upper bound (21--48\%).
6	Cross-architecture evaluation on Mistral-7B-v0.3 and Qwen-2.5-14B confirms PPL generalization with scale-dependent downstream effects.
7	This is primarily an empirical study; wall-clock speedup requires sparse kernel support not addressed here.
8	
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01LxwjdVi5tje11JqnZUhaWq`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Introduction}
2	
3	How the training objective shapes learned sparsity prediction quality is an open question for SwiGLU LLMs~\cite{shazeer2020glu}. SwiGLU FFN blocks exhibit high contextual sparsity, but their gated activation makes importance prediction challenging: neuron importance depends on both gate and up-projection values, a target that magnitude-based methods handle suboptimally.
4	Learned sparsity predictors~\cite{liu2023dejavu,gautam2026fastforward} exploit this property by training small networks to anticipate which neurons are important, but they predominantly rely on binary cross-entropy (BCE) loss, treating each neuron as an independent classification target.
5	Yet at inference, sparsity budgets are allocated globally: the TEAL (training-free activation sparsity;~\citealp{liu2025teal}) top-$k$ mechanism ranks all neurons across all layers and retains only those above a model-wide threshold.
6	This mismatch between per-neuron training and global inference allocation has not been formally analyzed.
7	While MaskLLM~\cite{fang2024maskllm} applies KL-based mask optimization, it targets static N:M weight sparsity; activation sparsity requires masks that generalize to unseen inputs, vary per token, and coordinate across layers through global allocation.
8	
9	Gradient analysis reveals a structural asymmetry: BCE's gradient $\partial \mathcal{L}_{\text{BCE}} / \partial s_j = \sigma(s_j) - m_j^*$ depends only on neuron $j$, while under KL divergence, the gradient couples each neuron to all others through the downstream Jacobian (\S\ref{sec:method:gradient}).
10	KL's coupled gradients discover neuron importance patterns that diverge 68\% from magnitude-based masks (per-layer Jaccard 32\%).
11	A target-swap ablation suggests that these discovered mask targets are the proximate quality driver at inference: BCE trained on KL-derived targets reproduces KL quality (max $\Delta$$\leq$0.79pp at 50\%, s42), while magnitude-trained BCE achieves 28.13$\pm$0.20. Cross-layer gradient coupling is not the primary driver of PPL-level quality, but contributes meaningfully to downstream task fidelity---a PPL--downstream dissociation quantified in \S\ref{sec:perlayer}.
12	
13	We instantiate a lightweight predictor (75.9M params, $<$1\% of base) trained with forward KL and Gumbel-Sigmoid relaxation~\cite{jang2017gumbel} on LLaMA-3.1-8B~\cite{grattafiori2024llama3}.
14	At 30\% sparsity under uniform allocation, the predictor reduces TEAL's perplexity by 14.5\% at matched sparsity (8.81 vs.\ 10.30 at 28.5\%) with stable downstream (MMLU 40.7$\pm$1.3\%, 3-seed); global top-$k$ amplifies cross-seed variance ($7.3\times$).
15	At 50\%, the perplexity advantage widens (50\% over TEAL), but downstream reasoning collapses regardless of method.
16	
17	Cross-architecture evaluation on Mistral-7B-v0.3~\cite{jiang2023mistral} (3-seed) confirms the PPL advantage ($-$17.3\%) with robust commonsense gains but tied MMLU; preliminary evaluation on Qwen-2.5-14B~\cite{qwen2024qwen25} shows consistent PPL improvement but reasoning decline (GSM8K $-$20pp, MMLU $-$2.8pp).
18	This paper is primarily an empirical study: we do not propose a new architecture or algorithm, but rather characterize how the training objective shapes sparsity prediction quality, and analyze interactions between allocation strategy, sparsity level, and model scale. The empirical finding---that output-distribution alignment is the critical design dimension for learned sparsity---is the paper's central contribution. Wall-clock speedup requires sparse kernel support; we do not address latency optimization (Appendix~\ref{app:latency}).
19	
20	Our contributions are:
21	\begin{itemize}[nosep,leftmargin=*]
22	\item An output-distribution alignment ranking across five losses, decomposed by a per-layer KL control into signal type (up to 97.5\% of PPL gap; upper bound, not fully orthogonal) and coupling scope (10--47\% on downstream tasks, $K{=}4$-corrected), with task-dependent downstream structure (\S\ref{sec:perlayer});
23	\item Exploratory cross-scale evidence at 7--14B confirming output-distribution losses outperform intermediate losses regardless of coupling scope (\S\ref{sec:experiments}).
24	\item A target-swap ablation suggests that KL's discovered mask targets drive inference-stage quality; cross-layer coupling refines downstream fidelity beyond what PPL captures (\S\ref{sec:perlayer}).
25	\item These mask patterns render post-hoc compensation (+67.2M params) redundant at both tested sparsity levels (\S\ref{sec:compensation}).
26	\end{itemize}
27	
28	\begin{figure}[t]
29	\centering
30	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{fig1_overview.pdf}
31	\caption{Method overview: a lightweight per-layer predictor produces importance scores trained via KL divergence with Gumbel-Sigmoid relaxation. At inference, scores feed TEAL's global top-$k$ allocation without compensation networks.}
32	\label{fig:overview}
33	\end{figure}
34
```

```json
      "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_14/projects/distill_sparse_swiglu/paper/introduction.tex",
      "content": "\\section{Introduction}\n\nHow the training objective shapes learned sparsity prediction quality is an open question for SwiGLU LLMs~\\cite{shazeer2020glu}. SwiGLU FFN blocks exhibit high contextual sparsity, but their gated activation makes importance prediction challenging: neuron importance depends on both gate and up-projection values, a target that magnitude-based methods handle suboptimally.\nLearned sparsity predictors~\\cite{liu2023dejavu,gautam2026fastforward} exploit this property by training small networks to anticipate which neurons are important, but they predominantly rely on binary cross-entropy (BCE) loss, treating each neuron as an independent classification target.\nYet at inference, sparsity budgets are allocated globally: the TEAL (training-free activation sparsity;~\\citealp{liu2025teal}) top-$k$ mechanism ranks all neurons across all layers and retains only those above a model-wide threshold.\nThis mismatch between per-neuron training and global inference allocation has not been formally analyzed.\nWhile MaskLLM~\\cite{fang2024maskllm} applies KL-based mask optimization, it targets static N:M weight sparsity; activation sparsity requires masks that generalize to unseen inputs, vary per token, and coordinate across layers through global allocation.\n\nGradient analysis reveals a structural asymmetry: BCE's gradient $\\partial \\mathcal{L}_{\\text{BCE}} / \\partial s_j = \\sigma(s_j) - m_j^*$ depends only on neuron $j$, while under KL divergence, the gradient couples each neuron to all others through the downstream Jacobian (\\S\\ref{sec:method:gradient}).\nKL's coupled gradients discover neuron importance patterns that diverge 68\\% from magnitude-based masks (per-layer Jaccard 32\\%).\nA target-swap ablation suggests that these discovered mask targets are the proximate quality driver at inference: BCE trained on KL-derived targets reproduces KL quality (max $\\Delta$$\\leq$0.79pp at 50\\%, s42), while magnitude-trained BCE achieves 28.13$\\pm$0.20. Cross-layer gradient coupling is not the primary driver of PPL-level quality, but contributes meaningfully to downstream task fidelity---a PPL--downstream dissociation quantified in \\S\\ref{sec:perlayer}.\n\nWe instantiate a lightweight predictor (75.9M params, $<$1\\% of base) trained with forward KL and Gumbel-Sigmoid relaxation~\\cite{jang2017gumbel} on LLaMA-3.1-8B~\\cite{grattafiori2024llama3}.\nAt 30\\% sparsity under uniform allocation, the predictor reduces TEAL's perplexity by 14.5\\% at matched sparsity (8.81 vs.\\ 10.30 at 28.5\\%) with stable downstream (MMLU 40.7$\\pm$1.3\\%, 3-seed); global top-$k$ amplifies cross-seed variance ($7.3\\times$).\nAt 50\\%, the perplexity advantage widens (50\\% over TEAL), but downstream reasoning collapses regardless of method.\n\nCross-architecture evaluation on Mistral-7B-v0.3~\\cite{jiang2023mistral} (3-seed) confirms the PPL advantage ($-$17.3\\%) with robust commonsense gains but tied MMLU; preliminary evaluation on Qwen-2.5-14B~\\cite{qwen2024qwen25} shows consistent PPL improvement but reasoning decline (GSM8K $-$20pp, MMLU $-$2.8pp).\nThis paper is primarily an empirical study: we do not propose a new architecture or algorithm, but rather characterize how the training objective shapes sparsity prediction quality, and analyze interactions between allocation strategy, sparsity level, and model scale. The empirical finding---that output-distribution alignment is the critical design dimension for learned sparsity---is the paper's central contribution. Wall-clock speedup requires sparse kernel support; we do not address latency optimization (Appendix~\\ref{app:latency}).\n\nOur contributions are:\n\\begin{itemize}[nosep,leftmargin=*]\n\\item An output-distribution alignment ranking across five losses, decomposed by a per-layer KL control into signal type (up to 97.5\\% of PPL gap; upper bound, not fully orthogonal) and coupling scope (10--47\\% on downstream tasks, $K{=}4$-corrected), with task-dependent downstream structure (\\S\\ref{sec:perlayer});\n\\item Exploratory cross-scale evidence at 7--14B confirming output-distribution losses outperform intermediate losses regardless of coupling scope (\\S\\ref{sec:experiments}).\n\\item A target-swap ablation suggests that KL's discovered mask targets drive inference-stage quality; cross-layer coupling refines downstream fidelity beyond what PPL captures (\\S\\ref{sec:perlayer}).\n\\item These mask patterns render post-hoc compensation (+67.2M params) redundant at both tested sparsity levels (\\S\\ref{sec:compensation}).\n\\end{itemize}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig1_overview.pdf}\n\\caption{Method overview: a lightweight per-layer predictor produces importance scores trained via KL divergence with Gumbel-Sigmoid relaxation. At inference, scores feed TEAL's global top-$k$ allocation without compensation networks.}\n\\label{fig:overview}\n\\end{figure}\n",
      "numLines": 34,
      "startLine": 1,
      "totalLines": 34
    }
  },
```

```text
1	\section{Conclusion}
2	\label{sec:conclusion}
3	
4	Output-distribution alignment is the dominant design dimension for PPL (signal type: up to 97.5\% of the gap at 30\%; upper bound, not fully orthogonal); cross-layer coupling contributes 10--47\% on downstream tasks (3-seed $K{=}4$-corrected; commonsense 22--47\%, GSM8K 10\%); MMLU anomalously favors $K{=}4$, reflecting allocation alignment confirmed by constraint analysis (Appendices~\ref{app:k4_downstream},~\ref{app:constraint}).
5	A predictor ($<$1\% of base) reduces TEAL's PPL by 14.5\% via masks 46--68\% divergent from magnitude; KL outperforms TEAL on commonsense (ARC-c, WG, BoolQ) but underperforms on knowledge tasks (MMLU, GSM8K) across tested scales---a learned allocation trade-off, not an inherent objective limitation (Appendix~\ref{app:constraint}).
6
```

```text
86	\paragraph{Target-Swap Ablation: Target Quality vs.\ Gradient Coupling.}
87	To disentangle whether KL's advantage stems from its coupled gradient pathway or from the mask patterns it discovers, we train a standard BCE predictor on KL-derived oracle masks instead of magnitude-based masks (C1 target-swap ablation).
88	Table~\ref{tab:c1} reports results under TEAL uniform allocation at matched sparsity.
89	
90	\begin{table}[t]
91	\centering
92	\caption{Target-swap ablation (C1): BCE on KL-derived masks vs.\ KL predictor (same seed), TEAL uniform. All seeds show max $\Delta$$\leq$1.70pp at 30\%; max $\Delta$$\leq$0.79pp at 50\% (s42). $^\dagger$s42 oracle is from an earlier training run; s123/s456 use matched retrained oracles.}
93	\label{tab:c1}
94	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{
95	\begin{tabular}{lllccccc}
96	\toprule
97	Sparsity & Seed & Method & PPL$\downarrow$ & ARC-c & WG & MMLU & GSM8K \\
98	\midrule
99	\multirow{6}{*}{30\%}
100	& s42$^\dagger$ & C1 & 9.45 & 40.27 & 67.40 & \textbf{42.30} & \textbf{7.58} \\ % exp_id: c1_ablation_30pct_s42_eval_v2
101	& & KL & \textbf{8.80} & \textbf{40.78} & \textbf{68.59} & 40.6 & 7.35 \\ % exp_id: bench_kl30_uniform
102	& s123 & C1 & 8.84 & 44.71 & \textbf{69.77} & \textbf{39.92} & 6.82 \\ % exp_id: eval_c1_retrain_30pct_s123
103	& & KL & \textbf{8.83} & \textbf{45.31} & 69.38 & 39.4 & \textbf{7.51} \\ % exp_id: kl30_s123_eval
104	& s456 & C1 & 8.82 & 44.71 & \textbf{69.93} & 41.04 & 6.60 \\ % exp_id: eval_c1_retrain_30pct_s456
105	& & KL & \textbf{8.79} & \textbf{45.05} & 68.75 & \textbf{42.0} & \textbf{7.58} \\ % exp_id: kl30_s456_uniform_eval
106	\midrule
107	\multirow{2}{*}{50\%}
108	& s42 & C1 & 11.96 & 32.17 & \textbf{63.14} & 27.79 & \textbf{2.05} \\ % exp_id: c1_bce_kl_targets_teal50u
109	& & KL & 11.96 & \textbf{32.59} & 62.35 & \textbf{27.92} & 1.97 \\ % exp_id: c1_kl_teal50u
110	\bottomrule
111	\end{tabular}}
112	\end{table}
113	
114	At 50\%, C1 matches KL within 0.79pp, while magnitude-trained BCE yields $2.4\times$ worse PPL (Table~\ref{tab:main}).
115	At 30\%, retrained seeds show $\leq$1.18pp agreement; the s42 oracle from an earlier checkpoint has a 0.65pp gap but supports the core finding.
116	C1 demonstrates that KL-discovered mask patterns \emph{transfer} their quality advantage to a simpler training mechanism (PPL within 0.79pp; downstream variation up to 4.5pp MMLU), consistent with masks as the proximate quality driver. However, KL's coupled optimization remains necessary for \emph{discovering} these patterns: a per-layer KL control (\S\ref{sec:perlayer}) shows that cross-layer coupling contributes marginally to PPL but substantially to downstream fidelity. % exp_id: bce50_3seed, c1_bce_kl_targets_30u, c1_s123
117	
118	\paragraph{Soft-vs-Hard Confound.}
119	C1 also changes mask representation (hard vs.\ soft), but BCE+Gumbel (32.22) vs.\ standard BCE (27.97) suggests softness alone does not explain KL's advantage; trajectory-dependent confounds are discussed in Appendix~\ref{app:oracle_provenance}. % exp_id: bce_gumbel_no_penalty_s42, eval_bce_v3_s42
```

```text
155	At 30\%, forward KL (8.81) and JSD (8.90) are near-identical, both outperforming reverse KL (9.26) and dramatically better than MSE (18.52$\pm$1.68; $\sigma$=1.68 vs.\ KL's $\sigma$=0.02, reflecting per-layer signal instability). % exp_id: kl30_ablation_s42, jsd30_ablation_s42, rkl30_ablation_s42, mse30_uniform_s42, mse30_3seed
156	The near-equivalence of KL and JSD suggests the divergence measure matters less than whether the loss optimizes through the output distribution; cross-layer scope provides additional downstream benefits (\S\ref{sec:perlayer}). % exp_id: bce30_uniform_s42, rkl30_downstream_3seed
157	\paragraph{Per-Layer KL: Decomposing Signal Type and Coupling Scope.}
158	\label{sec:perlayer}
159	To isolate cross-layer coupling, we train per-layer KL predictors that mask $K$ random layers per step and compute KL on the full model output, preserving the output-distribution signal without cross-layer gradients.
160	At $K{=}1$, per-layer KL achieves 9.05$\pm$0.02, within 2.7\% of end-to-end KL (8.81$\pm$0.02), while per-layer MSE degrades to 18.52$\pm$1.68 ($2.05\times$ worse). For PPL, signal type\footnote{We use ``signal type'' as shorthand for the training objective's optimization target (output distribution vs.\ intermediate activations); this dimension also entails differences in gradient flow topology and optimization landscape that are not independently controlled.} accounts for up to 97.5\% of the gap (upper bound; signal type and coupling scope are not fully orthogonal).
161	The $K{=}1$ downstream gap conflates true coupling with train--inference mismatch (training masks 1/32 layers; inference applies all 32). A 3-seed $K{=}4$ control\footnote{Actual sparsity 29.6\%; per-layer sparsity std=0.003 (near-uniform).} decomposes this confound (Appendix~\ref{app:k4_downstream}): \emph{coupling-dominated}---ARC-c (97\% residual gap) and WG (67\%) indicate genuine cross-layer benefits for commonsense; \emph{mismatch-dominated}---PPL (67\% closure) and GSM8K (71\%) gaps largely close; \emph{anomalous}---MMLU exceeds E2E across all seeds (42.7$\pm$1.4 vs.\ 40.7$\pm$1.3), explained by $K{=}4$'s near-uniform allocation matching uniform inference (Appendix~\ref{app:constraint}). % exp_id: perlayer_kl_30_3seed, perlayer_kl_downstream, mse30_downstream_decomp, perlayer_kl_k4_3seed
162	At 50\% ($K{=}1$ only), the gap widens sharply: per-layer KL degrades to 34.85 vs.\ 11.92 for E2E KL (+192\%), with coupling's share rising from 2.5\% to 10.6\% ($4\times$; Table~\ref{tab:ablation}) and downstream falling to TEAL level. % exp_id: perlayer_kl_downstream, perlayer_kl_50_s42
163	
164	\paragraph{STE Failure and Loss--Relaxation Interaction.}
165	\label{sec:ablation_ste}
166	At 30\%, STE yields only 20.8\% effective sparsity (vs.\ target 30\%), confirming Gumbel-Sigmoid is necessary. BCE+Gumbel (32.22) underperforms standard BCE (27.97); the loss effect ($\Delta$PPL=20.4) is $5.1\times$ the relaxation effect ($\Delta$PPL=3.98). % exp_id: ste_30pct_sparsity_failure, bce_gumbel_no_penalty_s42, bce_gumbel_lagrangian_isolation, eval_bce_v3_s42, bce_ste_s42
167	
168	\paragraph{Compensation Redundancy.}
169	\label{sec:compensation}
```

_source line 9394 · 2026-05-22T17:40:37.794Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01FAjvGeJTwWsRX1ZakkC4Fc`</summary>

```text
1	\section*{Limitations}
2	
3	Our findings are bounded by three key limitations.
4	\textbf{Sparsity sweet spot.} 30\% appears to be the lowest-degradation level for downstream fidelity; at 40\%, MMLU drops to 35.5\% (vs.\ 40.7\% at 30\%), and at 50\%, all methods collapse to near-random on reasoning tasks. A separate predictor must be retrained per target.
5	\textbf{Knowledge-intensive regression.} KL consistently underperforms magnitude-based methods on MMLU and GSM8K across scales (8B: $-$5.2pp MMLU; 14B: $-$20pp GSM8K), suggesting distribution-matching objectives systematically sacrifice reasoning-critical pathways.
6	\textbf{Training data homogeneity.} All predictors are trained on WikiText-103 (English Wikipedia). OOD evaluation on C4 shows preserved KL advantage but $31\times$ higher cross-seed variance (CV=6.3\% vs.\ 0.2\% in-domain), indicating domain sensitivity. Batched inference, where sparsity patterns must be intersected across sequences, may reduce effective sparsity and is not evaluated. Evaluation is limited to 7--14B; scaling to 70B+ and wall-clock speedup via sparse kernels remain future work.
7	
```

</details>

```text
85:\subsection{Adaptive Penalty Controller}
98:where $\epsilon = 0.02$ is a deadzone margin. The penalty weight $\lambda$ is updated via geometric doubling (doubled when sparsity exceeds target, halved otherwise), clamped to $[\lambda_{\text{floor}}, \lambda_{\max}]$, reaching target sparsity within 40--110 steps across all tested regimes (Appendix~\ref{app:lagrangian}).
```

```json
    "originalFile": "Learned sparsity predictors for SwiGLU LLMs predominantly use binary cross-entropy (BCE), training each neuron independently, yet inference allocates budgets globally via top-$k$ ranking.\nWe train lightweight predictors ($<$1\\% of base parameters) with forward KL divergence, coupling all mask decisions through the output distribution.\nOn LLaMA-3.1-8B at 30\\% sparsity, the KL predictor reduces perplexity by 14.5\\% over TEAL at matched sparsity, with commonsense gains but knowledge-intensive decline; post-hoc compensation (+67.2M params) becomes redundant.\nOutput-distribution optimization yields masks that diverge 46--68\\% from magnitude-based targets; a target-swap ablation suggests these discovered patterns---not the training mechanism itself---as the proximate quality driver.\nAn output-distribution alignment ranking emerges across five loss configurations; a per-layer KL control decomposes this into two dimensions: signal type dominates PPL (up to 97.5\\% of the gap at 30\\%; upper bound, as dimensions are not fully orthogonal), while coupling scope contributes 10--47\\% on downstream tasks (3-seed $K{=}4$-corrected; commonsense 22--47\\%, reasoning 10\\%); a train--inference mismatch confound in the $K{=}1$ control inflates the $K{=}1$ upper bound (21--48\\%).\nCross-architecture evaluation on Mistral-7B-v0.3 and Qwen-2.5-14B confirms PPL generalization with scale-dependent downstream effects.\nThis is primarily an empirical study; wall-clock speedup requires sparse kernel support not addressed here.\n",

{
```

```json
  "parentUuid": "df160a39-0911-4a01-aa59-04421e30ac35",

  "promptId": "7f54a02a-6e14-4eeb-961a-3dab1de3eec7",

  "toolUseResult": {
    "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_14/projects/distill_sparse_swiglu/paper/introduction.tex",
    "oldString": "A target-swap ablation suggests that these discovered mask targets are the proximate quality driver at inference:",
    "newString": "A target-swap ablation suggests that KL-discovered mask targets carry most of the quality information at inference:",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\nHow the training objective shapes learned sparsity prediction quality is an open question for SwiGLU LLMs~\\cite{shazeer2020glu}. SwiGLU FFN blocks exhibit high contextual sparsity, but their gated activation makes importance prediction challenging: neuron importance depends on both gate and up-projection values, a target that magnitude-based methods handle suboptimally.\nLearned sparsity predictors~\\cite{liu2023dejavu,gautam2026fastforward} exploit this property by training small networks to anticipate which neurons are important, but they predominantly rely on binary cross-entropy (BCE) loss, treating each neuron as an independent classification target.\nYet at inference, sparsity budgets are allocated globally: the TEAL (training-free activation sparsity;~\\citealp{liu2025teal}) top-$k$ mechanism ranks all neurons across all layers and retains only those above a model-wide threshold.\nThis mismatch between per-neuron training and global inference allocation has not been formally analyzed.\nWhile MaskLLM~\\cite{fang2024maskllm} applies KL-based mask optimization, it targets static N:M weight sparsity; activation sparsity requires masks that generalize to unseen inputs, vary per token, and coordinate across layers through global allocation.\n\nGradient analysis reveals a structural asymmetry: BCE's gradient $\\partial \\mathcal{L}_{\\text{BCE}} / \\partial s_j = \\sigma(s_j) - m_j^*$ depends only on neuron $j$, while under KL divergence, the gradient couples each neuron to all others through the downstream Jacobian (\\S\\ref{sec:method:gradient}).\nKL's coupled gradients discover neuron importance patterns that diverge 68\\% from magnitude-based masks (per-layer Jaccard 32\\%).\nA target-swap ablation suggests that these discovered mask targets are the proximate quality driver at inference: BCE trained on KL-derived targets reproduces KL quality (max $\\Delta$$\\leq$0.79pp at 50\\%, s42), while magnitude-trained BCE achieves 28.13$\\pm$0.20. Cross-layer gradient coupling is not the primary driver of PPL-level quality, but contributes meaningfully to downstream task fidelity---a PPL--downstream dissociation quantified in \\S\\ref{sec:perlayer}.\n\nWe instantiate a lightweight predictor (75.9M params, $<$1\\% of base) trained with forward KL and Gumbel-Sigmoid relaxation~\\cite{jang2017gumbel} on LLaMA-3.1-8B~\\cite{grattafiori2024llama3}.\nAt 30\\% sparsity under uniform allocation, the predictor reduces TEAL's perplexity by 14.5\\% at matched sparsity (8.81 vs.\\ 10.30 at 28.5\\%) with stable downstream (MMLU 40.7$\\pm$1.3\\%, 3-seed); global top-$k$ amplifies cross-seed variance ($7.3\\times$).\nAt 50\\%, the perplexity advantage widens (50\\% over TEAL), but downstream reasoning collapses regardless of method.\n\nCross-architecture evaluation on Mistral-7B-v0.3~\\cite{jiang2023mistral} (3-seed) confirms the PPL advantage ($-$17.3\\%) with robust commonsense gains but tied MMLU; preliminary evaluation on Qwen-2.5-14B~\\cite{qwen2024qwen25} shows consistent PPL improvement but reasoning decline (GSM8K $-$20pp, MMLU $-$2.8pp).\nThis paper is primarily an empirical study: we do not propose a new architecture or algorithm, but rather characterize how the training objective shapes sparsity prediction quality, and analyze interactions between allocation strategy, sparsity level, and model scale. The empirical finding---that output-distribution alignment is the critical design dimension for learned sparsity---is the paper's central contribution. Wall-clock speedup requires sparse kernel support; we do not address latency optimization (Appendix~\\ref{app:latency}).\n\nOur contributions are:\n\\begin{itemize}[nosep,leftmargin=*]\n\\item An output-distribution alignment ranking across five losses, decomposed by a per-layer KL control into signal type (up to 97.5\\% of PPL gap; upper bound, not fully orthogonal) and coupling scope (10--47\\% on downstream tasks, $K{=}4$-corrected), with task-dependent downstream structure (\\S\\ref{sec:perlayer});\n\\item Exploratory cross-scale evidence at 7--14B confirming output-distribution losses outperform intermediate losses regardless of coupling scope (\\S\\ref{sec:experiments}).\n\\item A target-swap ablation suggests that KL's discovered mask targets drive inference-stage quality; cross-layer coupling refines downstream fidelity beyond what PPL captures (\\S\\ref{sec:perlayer}).\n\\item These mask patterns render post-hoc compensation (+67.2M params) redundant at both tested sparsity levels (\\S\\ref{sec:compensation}).\n\\end{itemize}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig1_overview.pdf}\n\\caption{Method overview: a lightweight per-layer predictor produces importance scores trained via KL divergence with Gumbel-Sigmoid relaxation. At inference, scores feed TEAL's global top-$k$ allocation without compensation networks.}\n\\label{fig:overview}\n\\end{figure}\n",
    "structuredPatch": [
      {
        "oldStart": 8,
        "oldLines": 7,
        "newStart": 8,
        "newLines": 7,
        "lines": [
          " ",
          " Gradient analysis reveals a structural asymmetry: BCE's gradient $\\partial \\mathcal{L}_{\\text{BCE}} / \\partial s_j = \\sigma(s_j) - m_j^*$ depends only on neuron $j$, while under KL divergence, the gradient couples each neuron to all others through the downstream Jacobian (\\S\\ref{sec:method:gradient}).",
```

```json
    "oldString": "signal type (up to 97.5\\% of PPL gap; upper bound, not fully orthogonal) and coupling scope",
    "newString": "signal type (dominates PPL; upper bound) and coupling scope",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\nHow the training objective shapes learned sparsity prediction quality is an open question for SwiGLU LLMs~\\cite{shazeer2020glu}. SwiGLU FFN blocks exhibit high contextual sparsity, but their gated activation makes importance prediction challenging: neuron importance depends on both gate and up-projection values, a target that magnitude-based methods handle suboptimally.\nLearned sparsity predictors~\\cite{liu2023dejavu,gautam2026fastforward} exploit this property by training small networks to anticipate which neurons are important, but they predominantly rely on binary cross-entropy (BCE) loss, treating each neuron as an independent classification target.\nYet at inference, sparsity budgets are allocated globally: the TEAL (training-free activation sparsity;~\\citealp{liu2025teal}) top-$k$ mechanism ranks all neurons across all layers and retains only those above a model-wide threshold.\nThis mismatch between per-neuron training and global inference allocation has not been formally analyzed.\nWhile MaskLLM~\\cite{fang2024maskllm} applies KL-based mask optimization, it targets static N:M weight sparsity; activation sparsity requires masks that generalize to unseen inputs, vary per token, and coordinate across layers through global allocation.\n\nGradient analysis reveals a structural asymmetry: BCE's gradient $\\partial \\mathcal{L}_{\\text{BCE}} / \\partial s_j = \\sigma(s_j) - m_j^*$ depends only on neuron $j$, while under KL divergence, the gradient couples each neuron to all others through the downstream Jacobian (\\S\\ref{sec:method:gradient}).\nKL's coupled gradients discover neuron importance patterns that diverge 68\\% from magnitude-based masks (per-layer Jaccard 32\\%).\nA target-swap ablation suggests that KL-discovered mask targets carry most of the quality information at inference: BCE trained on KL-derived targets reproduces KL quality (max $\\Delta$$\\leq$0.79pp at 50\\%, s42), while magnitude-trained BCE achieves 28.13$\\pm$0.20. Cross-layer gradient coupling is not the primary driver of PPL-level quality, but contributes meaningfully to downstream task fidelity---a PPL--downstream dissociation quantified in \\S\\ref{sec:perlayer}.\n\nWe instantiate a lightweight predictor (75.9M params, $<$1\\% of base) trained with forward KL and Gumbel-Sigmoid relaxation~\\cite{jang2017gumbel} on LLaMA-3.1-8B~\\cite{grattafiori2024llama3}.\nAt 30\\% sparsity under uniform allocation, the predictor reduces TEAL's perplexity by 14.5\\% at matched sparsity (8.81 vs.\\ 10.30 at 28.5\\%) with stable downstream (MMLU 40.7$\\pm$1.3\\%, 3-seed); global top-$k$ amplifies cross-seed variance ($7.3\\times$).\nAt 50\\%, the perplexity advantage widens (50\\% over TEAL), but downstream reasoning collapses regardless of method.\n\nCross-architecture evaluation on Mistral-7B-v0.3~\\cite{jiang2023mistral} (3-seed) confirms the PPL advantage ($-$17.3\\%) with robust commonsense gains but tied MMLU; preliminary evaluation on Qwen-2.5-14B~\\cite{qwen2024qwen25} shows consistent PPL improvement but reasoning decline (GSM8K $-$20pp, MMLU $-$2.8pp).\nThis paper is primarily an empirical study: we do not propose a new architecture or algorithm, but rather characterize how the training objective shapes sparsity prediction quality, and analyze interactions between allocation strategy, sparsity level, and model scale. The empirical finding---that output-distribution alignment is the critical design dimension for learned sparsity---is the paper's central contribution. Wall-clock speedup requires sparse kernel support; we do not address latency optimization (Appendix~\\ref{app:latency}).\n\nOur contributions are:\n\\begin{itemize}[nosep,leftmargin=*]\n\\item An output-distribution alignment ranking across five losses, decomposed by a per-layer KL control into signal type (up to 97.5\\% of PPL gap; upper bound, not fully orthogonal) and coupling scope (10--47\\% on downstream tasks, $K{=}4$-corrected), with task-dependent downstream structure (\\S\\ref{sec:perlayer});\n\\item Exploratory cross-scale evidence at 7--14B confirming output-distribution losses outperform intermediate losses regardless of coupling scope (\\S\\ref{sec:experiments}).\n\\item A target-swap ablation suggests that KL's discovered mask targets drive inference-stage quality; cross-layer coupling refines downstream fidelity beyond what PPL captures (\\S\\ref{sec:perlayer}).\n\\item These mask patterns render post-hoc compensation (+67.2M params) redundant at both tested sparsity levels (\\S\\ref{sec:compensation}).\n\\end{itemize}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig1_overview.pdf}\n\\caption{Method overview: a lightweight per-layer predictor produces importance scores trained via KL divergence with Gumbel-Sigmoid relaxation. At inference, scores feed TEAL's global top-$k$ allocation without compensation networks.}\n\\label{fig:overview}\n\\end{figure}\n",
    "structuredPatch": [
      {
        "oldStart": 19,
        "oldLines": 7,
        "newStart": 19,
        "newLines": 7,
        "lines": [
          " ",
          " Our contributions are:",
          " \\begin{itemize}[nosep,leftmargin=*]",
          "-\\item An output-distribution alignment ranking across five losses, decomposed by a per-layer KL control into signal type (up to 97.5\\% of PPL gap; upper bound, not fully orthogonal) and coupling scope (10--47\\% on downstream tasks, $K{=}4$-corrected), with task-dependent downstream structure (\\S\\ref{sec:perlayer});",
          "+\\item An output-distribution alignment ranking across five losses, decomposed by a per-layer KL control into signal type (dominates PPL; upper bound) and coupling scope (10--47\\% on downstream tasks, $K{=}4$-corrected), with task-dependent downstream structure (\\S\\ref{sec:perlayer});",
          " \\item Exploratory cross-scale evidence at 7--14B confirming output-distribution losses outperform intermediate losses regardless of coupling scope (\\S\\ref{sec:experiments}).",
          " \\item A target-swap ablation suggests that KL's discovered mask targets drive inference-stage quality; cross-layer coupling refines downstream fidelity beyond what PPL captures (\\S\\ref{sec:perlayer}).",
          " \\item These mask patterns render post-hoc compensation (+67.2M params) redundant at both tested sparsity levels (\\S\\ref{sec:compensation})."
```

```json
    "originalFile": "\\section{Conclusion}\n\\label{sec:conclusion}\n\nOutput-distribution alignment is the dominant design dimension for PPL (signal type: up to 97.5\\% of the gap at 30\\%; upper bound, not fully orthogonal); cross-layer coupling contributes 10--47\\% on downstream tasks (3-seed $K{=}4$-corrected; commonsense 22--47\\%, GSM8K 10\\%); MMLU anomalously favors $K{=}4$, reflecting allocation alignment confirmed by constraint analysis (Appendices~\\ref{app:k4_downstream},~\\ref{app:constraint}).\nA predictor ($<$1\\% of base) reduces TEAL's PPL by 14.5\\% via masks 46--68\\% divergent from magnitude; KL outperforms TEAL on commonsense (ARC-c, WG, BoolQ) but underperforms on knowledge tasks (MMLU, GSM8K) across tested scales---a learned allocation trade-off, not an inherent objective limitation (Appendix~\\ref{app:constraint}).\n",
```
