        "lines": [
          " \\paragraph{Per-Layer KL: Decomposing Signal Type and Coupling Scope.}",
          " \\label{sec:perlayer}",
          " To isolate cross-layer coupling, we train per-layer KL predictors that mask $K$ random layers per step and compute KL on the full model output, preserving the output-distribution signal without cross-layer gradients.",
          "-At $K{=}1$, per-layer KL achieves 9.05$\\pm$0.02, within 2.7\\% of end-to-end KL (8.81$\\pm$0.02), while per-layer MSE degrades to 18.52$\\pm$1.68 ($2.05\\times$ worse). For PPL, signal type\\footnote{We use ``signal type'' as shorthand for the training objective's optimization target (output distribution vs.\\ intermediate activations); this dimension also entails differences in gradient flow topology and optimization landscape that are not independently controlled.} accounts for up to 97.5\\% of the gap (upper bound; signal type and coupling scope are not fully orthogonal).",
          "+At $K{=}1$, per-layer KL achieves 9.05$\\pm$0.02, within 2.7\\% of end-to-end KL (8.81$\\pm$0.02), while per-layer MSE degrades to 18.52$\\pm$1.68 ($2.05\\times$ worse). For PPL, signal type\\footnote{We use ``signal type'' as shorthand for the training objective's optimization target (output distribution vs.\\ intermediate activations); this dimension also entails differences in gradient flow topology and optimization landscape that are not independently controlled.} accounts for the majority of the gap. This is an upper bound: the $K{=}1$ control trains on 1/32 layers per step but infers on all 32, conflating coupling removal with train--inference mismatch. A $K{=}4$ control (Appendix~\\ref{app:k4_downstream}) partially deconfounds this.",
          " The $K{=}1$ downstream gap conflates true coupling with train--inference mismatch (training masks 1/32 layers; inference applies all 32). A 3-seed $K{=}4$ control\\footnote{Actual sparsity 29.6\\%; per-layer sparsity std=0.003 (near-uniform).} decomposes this confound (Appendix~\\ref{app:k4_downstream}): \\emph{coupling-dominated}---ARC-c (97\\% residual gap) and WG (67\\%) indicate genuine cross-layer benefits for commonsense; \\emph{mismatch-dominated}---PPL (67\\% closure) and GSM8K (71\\%) gaps largely close; \\emph{anomalous}---MMLU exceeds E2E across all seeds (42.7$\\pm$1.4 vs.\\ 40.7$\\pm$1.3), explained by $K{=}4$'s near-uniform allocation matching uniform inference (Appendix~\\ref{app:constraint}). % exp_id: perlayer_kl_30_3seed, perlayer_kl_downstream, mse30_downstream_decomp, perlayer_kl_k4_3seed",
          " At 50\\% ($K{=}1$ only), the gap widens sharply: per-layer KL degrades to 34.85 vs.\\ 11.92 for E2E KL (+192\\%), with coupling's share rising from 2.5\\% to 10.6\\% ($4\\times$; Table~\\ref{tab:ablation}) and downstream falling to TEAL level. % exp_id: perlayer_kl_downstream, perlayer_kl_50_s42",

  "new_string": "Per-layer constraint interventions (Appendix~\\ref{app:constraint}) confirm this is a task-dependent trade-off: removing the valley improves MMLU +3.3pp and GSM8K +1.5pp but reduces ARC-c by 4.4pp, consistent with findings that factual associations concentrate in specific FFN layers~\\cite{geva2023dissecting}.     "oldString": "An output-distribution alignment ranking emerges across five loss configurations; a per-layer KL control decomposes this into two dimensions: signal type dominates PPL (up to 97.5\\% of the gap at 30\\%; upper bound, as dimensions are not fully orthogonal), while coupling scope contributes 10--47\\% on downstream tasks (3-seed $K{=}4$-corrected; commonsense 22--47\\%, reasoning 10\\%); a train--inference mismatch confound in the $K{=}1$ control inflates the $K{=}1$ upper bound (21--48\\%).",
    "newString": "Signal type (output-distribution vs.\\ intermediate loss) accounts for the majority of the PPL gap at 30\\% (upper bound; not fully orthogonal with coupling scope).\nCross-layer coupling contributes 10--47\\% on downstream tasks (3-seed $K{=}4$-corrected; commonsense 22--47\\%, reasoning 10\\%).",
    "originalFile": "Learned sparsity predictors for SwiGLU LLMs predominantly use binary cross-entropy (BCE), training each neuron independently, yet inference allocates budgets globally via top-$k$ ranking.\nWe train lightweight predictors ($<$1\\% of base parameters) with forward KL divergence, coupling all mask decisions through the output distribution.\nOn LLaMA-3.1-8B at 30\\% sparsity, the KL predictor reduces perplexity by 14.5\\% over TEAL at matched sparsity, with commonsense gains but knowledge-intensive decline; post-hoc compensation (+67.2M params) becomes redundant.\nOutput-distribution optimization yields masks that diverge 46--68\\% from magnitude-based targets; a target-swap ablation suggests these discovered patterns carry most of the quality information: BCE trained on KL-derived targets approaches KL-level PPL (max $\\Delta$$\\leq$0.79pp, 3-seed).\nAn output-distribution alignment ranking emerges across five loss configurations; a per-layer KL control decomposes this into two dimensions: signal type dominates PPL (up to 97.5\\% of the gap at 30\\%; upper bound, as dimensions are not fully orthogonal), while coupling scope contributes 10--47\\% on downstream tasks (3-seed $K{=}4$-corrected; commonsense 22--47\\%, reasoning 10\\%); a train--inference mismatch confound in the $K{=}1$ control inflates the $K{=}1$ upper bound (21--48\\%).\nCross-architecture evaluation on Mistral-7B-v0.3 and Qwen-2.5-14B confirms PPL generalization with scale-dependent downstream effects.\nThis is primarily an empirical study; wall-clock speedup requires sparse kernel support not addressed here.\n",

          "-An output-distribution alignment ranking emerges across five loss configurations; a per-layer KL control decomposes this into two dimensions: signal type dominates PPL (up to 97.5\\% of the gap at 30\\%; upper bound, as dimensions are not fully orthogonal), while coupling scope contributes 10--47\\% on downstream tasks (3-seed $K{=}4$-corrected; commonsense 22--47\\%, reasoning 10\\%); a train--inference mismatch confound in the $K{=}1$ control inflates the $K{=}1$ upper bound (21--48\\%).",

          "+Limitations include MMLU/GSM8K regression across all tested scales, absence of wall-clock speedup, and evaluation bounded at 7--14B."

          " \\label{sec:perlayer}",
          " To isolate cross-layer coupling, we train per-layer KL predictors that mask $K$ random layers per step and compute KL on the full model output, preserving the output-distribution signal without cross-layer gradients.",
          "-At $K{=}1$, per-layer KL achieves 9.05$\\pm$0.02, within 2.7\\% of end-to-end KL (8.81$\\pm$0.02), while per-layer MSE degrades to 18.52$\\pm$1.68 ($2.05\\times$ worse).           "+At $K{=}1$, per-layer KL achieves 9.05$\\pm$0.02, within 2.7\\% of end-to-end KL (8.81$\\pm$0.02), while per-layer MSE degrades to 18.52$\\pm$1.68 ($2.05\\times$ worse).           " The $K{=}1$ downstream gap conflates true coupling with train--inference mismatch (training masks 1/32 layers; inference applies all 32). A 3-seed $K{=}4$ control\\footnote{Actual sparsity 29.6\\%; per-layer sparsity std=0.003 (near-uniform).} decomposes this confound (Appendix~\\ref{app:k4_downstream}): \\emph{coupling-dominated}---ARC-c (97\\% residual gap) and WG (67\\%) indicate genuine cross-layer benefits for commonsense; \\emph{mismatch-dominated}---PPL (67\\% closure) and GSM8K (71\\%) gaps largely close; \\emph{anomalous}---MMLU exceeds E2E across all seeds (42.7$\\pm$1.4 vs.\\ 40.7$\\pm$1.3), explained by $K{=}4$'s near-uniform allocation matching uniform inference (Appendix~\\ref{app:constraint}). % exp_id: perlayer_kl_30_3seed, perlayer_kl_downstream, mse30_downstream_decomp, perlayer_kl_k4_3seed",

   Hard constraint: EMNLP 8-page main text limit (abstract through conclusion). Limitations, ethics, references, appendix are unlimited.

2. Key Technical Concepts:
   - KL distillation vs BCE for SwiGLU activation sparsity prediction
   - Per-layer KL as 2D decomposition: signal type vs coupling scope
   - K=4 mismatch control: training masks K/32 layers, inference applies all 32
   - Three task-dependent categories: coupling-dominated (ARC-c 97%, WG 67%), mismatch-dominated (PPL 67%, GSM8K 71%), anomalous (MMLU >100%)
   - Per-layer sparsity constraint analysis: protect late vs remove valley interventions
   - C1 target-swap ablation: transferability (not causal exclusion)
   - 97.5% upper bound confounded by train-inference mismatch → changed to qualitative "majority"

3. Files and Code Sections:

   - **paper/abstract.tex**
     - Central claims modified across multiple patches
     - Current state after R22 Tier 1:
     ```
     Learned sparsity predictors for SwiGLU LLMs predominantly use binary cross-entropy (BCE), training each neuron independently, yet inference allocates budgets globally via top-$k$ ranking.
     We train lightweight predictors ($<$1\% of base parameters) with forward KL divergence, coupling all mask decisions through the output distribution.
     On LLaMA-3.1-8B at 30\% sparsity, the KL predictor reduces perplexity by 14.5\% over TEAL at matched sparsity, with commonsense gains but knowledge-intensive decline; post-hoc compensation (+67.2M params) becomes redundant.
     Output-distribution optimization yields masks that diverge 46--68\% from magnitude-based targets; a target-swap ablation suggests these discovered patterns carry most of the quality information: BCE trained on KL-derived targets approaches KL-level PPL (max $\Delta$\leq$0.79pp, 3-seed).
     Signal type (output-distribution vs.\ intermediate loss) accounts for the majority of the PPL gap at 30\% (upper bound; not fully orthogonal with coupling scope).
     Cross-layer coupling contributes 10--47\% on downstream tasks (3-seed $K{=}4$-corrected; commonsense 22--47\%, reasoning 10\%).
     Cross-architecture evaluation on Mistral-7B-v0.3 and Qwen-2.5-14B confirms PPL generalization with scale-dependent downstream effects.
     This is primarily an empirical study; wall-clock speedup requires sparse kernel support not addressed here.
     ```

     Output-distribution alignment is the dominant design dimension for PPL (signal type accounts for the majority of the gap; upper bound, confounded by train--inference mismatch in the $K{=}1$ control); cross-layer coupling contributes 10--47\% on downstream tasks (3-seed $K{=}4$-corrected; commonsense 22--47\%, GSM8K 10\%); MMLU anomalously favors $K{=}4$, reflecting allocation alignment confirmed by constraint analysis (Appendices~\ref{app:k4_downstream},~\ref{app:constraint}).
     A predictor ($<$1\% of base) reduces TEAL's PPL by 14.5\% via masks 46--68\% divergent from magnitude; KL outperforms TEAL on commonsense (ARC-c, WG, HellaSwag, PIQA) but underperforms on knowledge tasks (MMLU, GSM8K) across tested scales---a learned allocation trade-off, not an inherent objective limitation (Appendix~\ref{app:constraint}).
     Limitations include MMLU/GSM8K regression across all tested scales, absence of wall-clock speedup, and evaluation bounded at 7--14B.

{
  "parentUuid": "3a8b654b-bc05-4932-88e3-db3e4892bef3",
  "isSidechain": false,
  "attachment": {
    "type": "file",
    "filename": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_14/projects/distill_sparse_swiglu/paper/introduction.tex",
    "content": {
      "type": "text",
      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_14/projects/distill_sparse_swiglu/paper/introduction.tex",
        "content": "\\section{Introduction}\n\nHow the training objective shapes learned sparsity prediction quality is an open question for SwiGLU LLMs~\\cite{shazeer2020glu}. SwiGLU FFN blocks exhibit high contextual sparsity, but their gated activation makes importance prediction challenging: neuron importance depends on both gate and up-projection values, a target that magnitude-based methods handle suboptimally.\nLearned sparsity predictors~\\cite{liu2023dejavu,gautam2026fastforward} exploit this property by training small networks to anticipate which neurons are important, but they predominantly rely on binary cross-entropy (BCE) loss, treating each neuron as an independent classification target.\nYet at inference, sparsity budgets are allocated globally: the TEAL (training-free activation sparsity;~\\citealp{liu2025teal}) top-$k$ mechanism ranks all neurons across all layers and retains only those above a model-wide threshold.\nThis mismatch between per-neuron training and global inference allocation has not been formally analyzed.\nWhile MaskLLM~\\cite{fang2024maskllm} applies KL-based mask optimization, it targets static N:M weight sparsity; activation sparsity requires masks that generalize to unseen inputs, vary per token, and coordinate across layers through global allocation.\n\nGradient analysis reveals a structural asymmetry: BCE's gradient $\\partial \\mathcal{L}_{\\text{BCE}} / \\partial s_j = \\sigma(s_j) - m_j^*$ depends only on neuron $j$, while under KL divergence, the gradient couples each neuron to all others through the downstream Jacobian (\\S\\ref{sec:method:gradient}).\nKL's coupled gradients discover neuron importance patterns that diverge 68\\% from magnitude-based masks (per-layer Jaccard 32\\%).\nA target-swap ablation suggests that KL-discovered mask targets carry most of the quality information at inference: BCE trained on KL-derived targets reproduces KL quality (max $\\Delta$$\\leq$0.79pp at 50\\%, s42), while magnitude-trained BCE achieves 28.13$\\pm$0.20. Cross-layer gradient coupling is not the primary driver of PPL-level quality, but contributes meaningfully to downstream task fidelity---a PPL--downstream dissociation quantified in \\S\\ref{sec:perlayer}.\n\nWe instantiate a lightweight predictor (75.9M params, $<$1\\% of base) trained with forward KL and Gumbel-Sigmoid relaxation~\\cite{jang2017gumbel} on LLaMA-3.1-8B~\\cite{grattafiori2024llama3}.\nAt 30\\% sparsity under uniform allocation, the predictor reduces TEAL's perplexity by 14.5\\% at matched sparsity (8.81 vs.\\ 10.30 at 28.5\\%) with stable downstream (MMLU 40.7$\\pm$1.3\\%, 3-seed); global top-$k$ amplifies cross-seed variance ($7.3\\times$).\nAt 50\\%, the perplexity advantage widens (50\\% over TEAL), but downstream reasoning collapses regardless of method.\n\nCross-architecture evaluation on Mistral-7B-v0.3~\\cite{jiang2023mistral} (3-seed) confirms the PPL advantage ($-$17.3\\%) with robust commonsense gains but tied MMLU; preliminary evaluation on Qwen-2.5-14B~\\cite{qwen2024qwen25} shows consistent PPL improvement but reasoning decline (GSM8K $-$20pp, MMLU $-$2.8pp).\nThis paper is primarily an empirical study: we do not propose a new architecture or algorithm, but rather characterize how the training objective shapes sparsity prediction quality, and analyze interactions between allocation strategy, sparsity level, and model scale. The empirical finding---that output-distribution alignment is the critical design dimension for learned sparsity---is the paper's central contribution. Wall-clock speedup requires sparse kernel support; we do not address latency optimization (Appendix~\\ref{app:latency}).\n\nOur contributions are:\n\\begin{itemize}[nosep,leftmargin=*]\n\\item An output-distribution alignment ranking across five losses, decomposed by a per-layer KL control into signal type (dominates PPL; upper bound) and coupling scope (10--47\\% on downstream tasks, $K{=}4$-corrected), with task-dependent downstream structure (\\S\\ref{sec:perlayer});\n\\item Exploratory cross-scale evidence at 7--14B confirming output-distribution losses outperform intermediate losses regardless of coupling scope (\\S\\ref{sec:experiments}).\n\\item A target-swap ablation suggests that KL's discovered mask targets drive inference-stage quality; cross-layer coupling refines downstream fidelity beyond what PPL captures (\\S\\ref{sec:perlayer}).\n\\item These mask patterns render post-hoc compensation (+67.2M params) redundant at both tested sparsity levels (\\S\\ref{sec:compensation}).\n\\end{itemize}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig1_overview.pdf}\n\\caption{Method overview: a lightweight per-layer predictor produces importance scores trained via KL divergence with Gumbel-Sigmoid relaxation. At inference, scores feed TEAL's global top-$k$ allocation without compensation networks.}\n\\label{fig:overview}\n\\end{figure}\n",
        "numLines": 34,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 34
      }
    },
    "displayPath": "../../../paper/introduction.tex"
  },
  "type": "attachment",

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01Aj4cxQ4zWettjG9RajfVSi`</summary>

```text
1	\section*{Limitations}
2	
3	Our findings are bounded by three key limitations.
4	\textbf{Sparsity sweet spot.} 30\% appears to be the lowest-degradation level for downstream fidelity; at 40\%, MMLU drops to 35.5\% (vs.\ 40.7\% at 30\%), and at 50\%, all methods collapse to near-random on reasoning tasks. A separate predictor must be retrained per target.
5	\textbf{Knowledge-intensive regression.} KL consistently underperforms magnitude-based methods on MMLU and GSM8K across scales (8B: $-$5.2pp MMLU; 14B: $-$20pp GSM8K), suggesting distribution-matching objectives systematically sacrifice reasoning-critical pathways.
6	\textbf{Training data homogeneity.} All predictors are trained on WikiText-103 (English Wikipedia). OOD evaluation on C4 shows preserved KL advantage but $31\times$ higher cross-seed variance (CV=6.3\% vs.\ 0.2\% in-domain), indicating domain sensitivity. Batched inference, where sparsity patterns must be intersected across sequences, may reduce effective sparsity and is not evaluated. Evaluation is limited to 7--14B; scaling to 70B+ and wall-clock speedup via sparse kernels remain future work.
7	
```

</details>
